
### Paper [1]
Title: Strain-transfer at the Cu nanoparticle-Si(111) interface: static atomistic modelling and simulated Bragg CDI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28036
Authors: Corentin Chatelier
Abstract: Substrate strain offers a route to control deformation in supported metallic nanoparticles, provided that interfacial coupling transmits the imposed loading into the particle. We assess this feasibility using five static Cu/Si(111) configurations spanning 0-2% biaxial tensile substrate strain. A 211,288-atom Cu particle is supported by a nominally 50 x 50 x 20 nm Si slab with its bottom 1 nm fixed, using the Jelinek modified embeddedatom potential. Relative to the relaxed supported 0% state, Cu-only local fits reveal heterogeneous, anisotropic deformation: particle-wide mean hydrostatic strain is negative at every nonzero loading, from -3.12 x 10-6 to -8.84 x 10-6, while the upper-particle mean varies nonmonotonically. Simulated Bragg CDI of Cu(111), obtained by inversion of known complex atomic scattering amplitudes, maps loading-dependent projected displacement and strain. The combined atomistic and Bragg CDI representations provide strong preliminary support for the feasibility of substrate-mediated strain transfer in this idealized system and identify spatial signatures for experimental investigation. Experiments remain indispensable to confirm transfer in real Cu/Si(111) nanoparticles and determine its magnitude.

### Paper [2]
Title: From raw magnetoresistance to reliable cyclotron masses: A practical guide to processing quantum oscillation data
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28044
Authors: Josh Leeman, Sudipta Chatterjee, Max Hirschberger, Leslie M. Schoop
Abstract: Quantum oscillations are an essential probe of the Fermi surface in conductors, and cyclotron masses extracted using the Lifshitz-Kosevich (LK) formula are used to evaluate correlation strength and the assignment of topological states. These masses depend on choices in signal processing, including the field window, method of background subtraction, and spectral window applied before the Fourier transform. These choices are often guided by visual inspection, yet they can alter spectral features and bias the extracted masses. We show that (1) representing a finite field interval by a single effective field can underestimate the cyclotron mass; (2) polynomial background subtraction can attenuate and shift low-frequency peaks; and (3) spectral tapering can reduce mass bias from leakage between neighboring peaks, at the cost of frequency resolution. We demonstrate these effects using numerical calculations and Shubnikov-de Haas experiments on the rare-earth tritellurides RTe3 (R = Pr, Nd, Gd, Tb) with a dense low-frequency spectrum in the charge ordered state. The examples reveal masses shifting by tens of percent with the field window, apparent peak shifts produced only by the background fit, and a reported departure from LK behavior that resolves into two neighboring frequencies.

### Paper [3]
Title: Particle separation in a time-correlated Gaussian velocity field: a numerical study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28147
Authors: Mario Maurelli, Silvia Morlacchi
Abstract: We numerically investigate the dispersion of passive tracer pairs in a two-dimensional, divergence-free, time-correlated Gaussian velocity field. The field is constructed from a sparse set of Fourier modes evolving as independent Ornstein--Uhlenbeck processes, with spatial regularity and scale-dependent temporal decorrelation comparable to those of a power-law Gaussian velocity ensemble. We show that, for spatial roughness exponent $\alpha=0.2$ and temporal decorrelation exponent $\beta=0.5$, corresponding to the regime $\alpha+\beta<1$, the extrapolated mean-square distance is compatible with particle separation (in mean-square) at positive time: two particles reach a mean-square positive distance at a finite time, no matter how close they are initially.

### Paper [4]
Title: Structural, optoelectronic, mechanical, thermodynamic and thermoelectric properties of kesterite Cu2ZnSnS4: A first-principles study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28172
Authors: Iskandar Raufzoda, Amondulloi Burhonzoda, Dilshod Nematov, Makhmadzoir Kayumov, Saidjafar Murodzoda, Anushervon Ashurov, Sakhidod Sattorzoda, Makhsud Barot Islomzoda, Kholmirzo Kholmurodov, Shuwei Tang
Abstract: The structural, optoelectronic, mechanical, thermodynamic, and thermoelectric properties of kesterite Cu2ZnSnS4 were investigated using density functional theory. The mBJ and HSE06 approaches yield band gaps of 0.88 and 1.55 eV, respectively. Optical calculations reveal high absorption, with the onset in the 0.8-1.0 eV range. Hydrostatic compression from 0 to 30 kbar decreases the lattice parameters and increases the band gap. The elastic constants satisfy the Born stability criterion, and B/G $>$ 1.75 indicates plastic behavior. Phonon calculations confirm dynamic stability. In the temperature range from 0 to 1000 K, the Helmholtz free energy decreases, the vibrational entropy increases, and the heat capacity approaches a plateau above 500 K. The thermoelectric properties were investigated in the temperature range from 100 to 1000 K. The Seebeck coefficient remains positive, peaking at approximately 310 $\mu$V/K at 250 K and decreasing to 239 $\mu$V/K at 1000 K. These results encourage the investigation of Cu2ZnSnS4 as a good candidate for thermoelectric power conversion.

### Paper [5]
Title: Physically Informed Data-Driven Modeling of Low-Pressure Carburizing (LPC): A Self-Calibrating Boundary Condition for Carbon Flux
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28190
Authors: Alex V. Sagalovych, Tymofii Sagalovych, S. F. Dudnik, Vladislav Sagalovych
Abstract: This paper formulates an extended framework for a hybrid physically informed data-driven model of low-pressure carburizing (LPC), in which diffusive carbon transport in austenite is described by a physical equation, whereas the effective carbon flux across the gas-metal interface is treated as an unknown boundary forcing to be identified from experimental and process data. The basis for this formulation is that, in LPC, the most uncertain part of the model is not bulk diffusion but the transient surface kinetics of carbon uptake by the metal. In contrast to direct empirical relationships of the 'process regime-result' type, the proposed scheme uses machine learning as a closure operator for the boundary condition in the diffusion problem. This operator is calibrated using carbon concentration profiles, integral mass gain, surface concentration, effective case depth, and additional process measurements. Theoretically, the model is positioned as an operator-learning problem in a system constrained by a partial differential equation, physical flux constraints, inverse-problem regularization, and uncertainty quantification. The relationships between the proposed approach and physics-informed neural networks, operator learning, dynamic-system identification, boundary-operator learning, and boundary-control problems are considered. The mathematical formulation, calibration functional, identifiability conditions, preliminary tuning algorithm, and online adaptation scheme are formulated. The proposed approach is considered as a computational basis for developing interpretable digital support for LPC processes.

### Paper [6]
Title: Defect Engineering of the Water-Carbon Interface: Insights from First-Principles Calculations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28201
Authors: Peikun Zhang, Hongbo Zhang, Ying Xu, Zhuhua Zhang
Abstract: Interactions at solid-liquid interfaces are fundamental to hydrovoltaic and catalytic technologies. While carbon nanomaterials have been extensively studied, the role of abundant defects in amorphous carbon remains poorly understood. Here, using first-principles calculations, we systematically investigate how various defects (topological, point, line, edge, and doping defects) modulate water-carbon interactions. We find that armchair-edge defects with dangling bonds reduce the energy barrier for water dissociation by up to 30 times compared with pristine carbon, while di-vacancies exhibit a near-zero Gibbs free energy for hydrogen evolution, enabling potential hydrogen evolution reaction activity on a purely carbon-based interface. Moreover, mono-vacancy defects, characterized by reduced electronic localization, enhance charge transfer to evaporating water molecules by up to 12.4 times, a key factor in evaporation-induced electricity generation. Crucially, we demonstrate that coupling distinct defects produces synergistic effects that outperform individual defects, revealing a promising strategy for designing multifunctional interfaces. These insights establish a defect-engineering framework for the rational design of high-efficiency hydrovoltaic and catalytic devices.

### Paper [7]
Title: Multiorbital driving of quantum geometric nonlinear transport in oxide interfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28213
Authors: Irene Gaiardoni, Mattia Trama, Roberta Citro, Annika Johansson
Abstract: Quantum geometry plays a relevant role in determining the intrinsic non-linear transport properties of low-dimensional electron systems. Here, we theoretically investigate the quantum geometric contribution to the non-linear conductivity in $\text{SrTiO}_3$-based two-dimensional electron gases, comparing the prototypical (001) and (111) crystallographic interface orientations. Using a multi-orbital tight-binding approach, we uncover the crucial role of the multiband electronic structure in determining the quantum-geometric contribution to nonlinear transport. In particular, we show how the hybridization of $t_{2g}$ orbitals near avoided band crossings shapes the momentum-space distribution of the band-normalized quantum metric and the resulting nonlinear conductivity. Furthermore, we reveal a strong dependence on crystal orientation: while the (001) interface exhibits sharp peak-and-dip features confined around Lifshitz transitions, the trigonal symmetry of the (111) interface promotes strong orbital mixing. Our results highlight how the interplay between orbital hybridization and crystallographic orientation gives rise to distinct quantum-geometric nonlinear transport responses in oxide interfaces.

### Paper [8]
Title: Nonequilibrium fluctuations could explain the hot mitochondria paradox
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28225
Authors: Matthew P. Leighton, Benjamin B. Machta
Abstract: Several different experiments have found apparent temperature differences of up to 10 K between the interior of mitochondria and the rest of the cellular environment. The physics of heat conduction, however, precludes the steady-state existence of a temperature difference greater than the microkelvin scale within the cell, absent a far greater energy budget. Nearly a decade after the first reported measurement, this apparent paradox remains unexplained. Here, we propose an explanation which allows the elevated temperature measurements to coexist with the theoretical impossibility of a true 10 K steady-state temperature difference. We suggest that nonequilibrium fluctuations within the cellular environment, combined with molecular temperature probes sensitive to specific ranges of frequencies, can lead to apparent 10 K temperature differences with a biologically attainable power budget.

### Paper [9]
Title: Metal to Semiconductor Transition in Niobium Sulfoselenide Alloy Films by Compositional Control
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28238
Authors: Tinsae Alem, Abir Hasan, Jordan A. Hachtel, Kory Burns, Nikhil Shukla, Stephen McDonnell
Abstract: Transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) have garnered significant interest due to their tunable bandgaps, optical anisotropy, and mechanical flexibility. This study explores the relationship between stoichiometry, structural phase, and electronic transport in niobium sulfoselenide (NbSxSe2-x) thin films. In this study, low-dimensional TMD films were grown with molecular beam epitaxy (MBE) to investigate the effects of varying chalcogen (sulfur and selenium) content in NbSxSe2-x alloys. We focus on tuning electrical resistivity along with the transition from metallic-to-semiconducting behavior through compositional control. These MBE grown films were characterized using in-vacuo x-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS) to analyze the chemical composition. Electrical resistivity of the films and their temperature coefficient of resistance (TCR) were extracted to determine their metallic and semiconducting behavior. Our results show that increasing sulfur content induces a transition from metallic to semiconducting behavior in NbSe2, accompanied by a rise in resistivity. This work highlights the potential for compositional tuning of TMDs for applications in tunable nanoelectronics.

### Paper [10]
Title: Symmetry classes of hyperdeterminant wavefunctions: Application to phase transitions between a bosonic fractional Chern insulator and superfluids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28276
Authors: Guan-Lin Lin, Xiaodong Hu, Di Xiao, Ying Ran
Abstract: We study a phase transition between a superfluid phase and a fractional Chern insulator phase in a hard-core boson model on the honeycomb lattice using symmetric hyperdeterminant (Hdet) wavefunctions. Combining projective symmetry group analysis with variational Monte Carlo, we construct and optimize Hdet states in various symmetry classes. Based on the associated effective theories, we show that different symmetry classes, i.e., representations of the projective symmetry group, describe specific quantum phases. Compared with traditional fermionic parton construction, the symmetric Hdet wavefunctions are shown to improve microscopics significantly, allowing quantitative investigation of quantum phase diagrams. We argue that, in contrast with the common belief, this putative continuous phase transition is described by a fluctuating $SU(2)_g$ gauge field coupling with four Dirac nodes forming two $SU(2)_g$ doublets (i.e., $N_f=2$ QCD).

### Paper [11]
Title: First-Principles Investigation of the {ScSe2} TMDs Monolayer: Structural Stability, Electronic and Optical Properties
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28279
Authors: Takwa Ben Othman, Mohsen Jema\"i, Said Ridene
Abstract: We present an ab initio investigation of the {ScSe2} monolayer using density functional theory (DFT), as employed in the CP2K code, to assess its structural stability and electronic properties. The electronic behavior of both phases is governed by the local coordination environment of scandium and the associated lifting of degeneracy of its $3d$ orbitals. The valence bands (VBs) are dominated by {Se}$-4p$ states in both phases, while the states near the Fermi level distinguish the metallic 1T phase from the semiconducting 2H phase. Optical response calculations reveal markedly different spectral signatures for the two polymorphs. Two findings emerge from this study: a polymorphism-driven metal-to-semiconductor transition without any change in chemical composition and distinct optical responses relevant to phase-selective optoelectronic devices.

### Paper [12]
Title: Intrinsic volume fraction can decouple from hydrodynamic size in p(NIPAm-co-AA) microgels
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28284
Authors: Peter Edimeh, Howard Chen, Jacinta C. Conrad
Abstract: Quantifying the packing fraction of microgels is key to understanding their rheology and transport properties. Often, packing fractions are extrapolated from the hydrodynamic size and intrinsic volume fraction under the assumption that particle size and polymer volume change are proportional. Here, we test this assumption for p(N-isopropylacrylamide-co-acrylic acid) [p(NIPAm-co-AA)] microgels of two crosslinker concentrations (1 wt\% and 7 wt\% methylenebisacrylamide) using capillary viscometry and light scattering (static and dynamic) above and below the pKa of acrylic acid (AA). At pH 8, where AA is fully ionized, the microgels do not undergo a pronounced volume phase transition because electrostatic repulsion hinders thermal collapse, and both measures of hydrodynamic volume are equal. At pH 4, however, p(NIPAm-co-AA) microgels are weakly charged and exhibit a volume phase transition near the lower critical solution temperature of pNIPAm; the magnitude of the size change is greater for the less-crosslinked microgel. Above the volume phase transition temperature, the volume change from hydrodynamic radius decouples from that from viscosity. The ratio of the radius of gyration and hydrodynamic radius R_g/R_h of p(NIPAm-co-AA) microgels at high temperature exceeds the value theoretically predicted for homogeneous spheres and the deviation increases with the extent of crosslinking, suggesting that a non-uniform internal distribution of polymer gives rise to the anomalous decoupling at high temperatures. Our results indicate that care must be taken in extrapolating packing fractions for multi-responsive copolymer microgels, and suggest that copolymer composition and crosslink density may be used to tune bulk properties such as rheology through the internal microstructure.

### Paper [13]
Title: Oscillatory superconducting proximity effects in a buried Pt interlayer detected by FMR
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28313
Authors: L. Peridon, T. Courtois, S. A Hussain, J. B. Tjernshaugen, J. Linder, S. ElMeligy, B. \'Ujfalussy, K. Dumesnil, S. Mangin, S. Petit-Watelot, C. Ciccarelli, T. Hauet
Abstract: We investigate superconducting proximity effects in NbN/Pt/Py and NbN/Cu/Py trilayers using broadband ferromagnetic resonance (FMR). The reduction of the spin-pumping contribution to the Py damping below the superconducting transition temperature (Tc) is used to probe the superconducting correlations and electronic density of state in the buried metallic interlayer. For a Cu interlayer, the damping reduction remains finite up to at least 20 nm, in agreement with the large coherence length above 100nm in Cu. In contrast, the damping reduction for a Pt interlayer exhibits a non-monotonic thickness dependence and is fully suppressed above 5 nm. Using the quasiclassical theory, we are able to explain the oscillation in damping reduction below Tc with the coexistence of superconducting and magnetic proximity effects in Pt, which generates oscillations of the superconducting order parameter and electronic density of state in the exchange-splitted Pt interlayer. Our results establish spin-pumping damping as a sensitive probe of superconducting proximity effects in buried metallic layers.

### Paper [14]
Title: Wireless readout and control of cavity magnon-polaritons via photonic modes delocalised across a gap
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28347
Authors: Alban Joseph, Ryan D McKenzie, Amy Eden, Morven Last, Shannon Howard, Alzbeta Oravcova, Mawgan A Smith, Robert L Stamps, Rair Macedo
Abstract: In cavity magnonics, photon-magnon hybridisation is typically detected by measuring the cavity response (i.e. measuring cavity resonance shifts through its reflection or transmission spectra). While a large body of work in the field has focused on three-dimensional cavities, a variety of two-dimensional resonators have also been explored as hosts for photonic modes due to their compatibility with on-chip integration. Nevertheless, the readout approach remains largely unchanged. Here, we demonstrate remote excitation and readout of cavity magnon-polaritons using two spatially separated, wirelessly coupled ring resonators. Through inductive coupling, energy is transferred between the resonators, enabling the magnonic system to be excited by one resonator while its hybrid-mode spectrum is detected through measurements of the other. We demonstrate wireless readout of cavity magnon-polaritons at separations ranging from millimetres to centimetres, identifying their characteristic response in the remotely measured spectrum. This establishes wireless resonator coupling as a route to remote interrogation of hybrid photon-magnon systems.

### Paper [15]
Title: Exploring new speciation patterns: architecture of genetic similarity networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28358
Authors: David M. Schneider, Claudio Pastorino
Abstract: We use an individual based model to identify signatures of speciation modes in genetic similarity networks. These networks group individuals based on their genetic similarity, defined through Hamming distances between binary strings representing their genomes. The results of our extensive simulations demonstrate that sympatric and parapatric speciation modes are clearly distinguishable and measurable through specific network metrics. In particular, structural properties -- including clustering coefficient, mean degree, geodesic distance, and assortativity -- show significant differences in both stationary values and transient phases, giving rise to a novel framework for distinguishing between speciation modes.

### Paper [16]
Title: Intertwined Magneto-Structural Criticality Driving Heterogeneous Collective-Pseudospin Dynamics in Sr2IrO4
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28395
Authors: Arnab Singh, Junyoung Kwon, AM Milinda Abeykoon
Abstract: Correlated quantum phenomena arising from spin-orbit-coupled degrees of freedom provide foundational paradigms in emerging quantum technologies and spin-based functionalities. Elucidating the cooperative behaviors of these systems requires a precise probing of their intertwined magnetic and structural configurations. Using complementary resonant X-ray diffraction and high-energy total scattering measurements on single crystals of Sr2IrO4, we observe that spontaneous structural distortions scale quadratically with the magnetic order parameter. Importantly, the lattice distortion inherits the characteristic 2D XY -like critical behavior of the underlying antiferromagnetic order. X-ray photon correlation spectroscopy (XPCS) reveals multiscale, temperature- and wave-vector-dependent magnetic dynamics characterized by compressed-exponential relaxation and signatures of critical slowing down near the magnetic transition. These findings suggest the role of spin-orbit coupling and local structural distortions as the possible mechanism orchestrating such unconventional dynamics.

### Paper [17]
Title: A Covalent Bonding Network Using p-Block Elements to Stabilize Lithium-Rich Cathode Materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28452
Authors: Amelia Zhao, Edward Mu, Jessica Luu, John Vinson, Brian Moritz, Eder G. Lomeli, Thomas P. Devereaux
Abstract: Li-rich battery cathodes (LixMyOz, where x > y) have sparked significant attention for their high energy densities, but their practical application is currently limited by structural instabilities associated with high-valent redox. Recent work has demonstrated reversible high-potential (de)lithiation with low voltage hysteresis in Li4FeSbO6 (LFSO), but the stabilization mechanism for this unique charging behavior is not fully understood, limiting our ability to apply insights from LFSO to more earth-abundant cathodes. In this paper, we present a covalent Sb-O bonding network in LFSO which is facilitated by the p-block orbital character of Sb. Using ab initio computational modeling, we identify this bonding state and indicate its contributions to crystal stability; we also identify a signature for this bonding network using theoretical and experimental element-specific X-ray spectroscopy. Substituting Sb with other p-block elements preserves this stabilizing bond, pointing to a family of new cathode chemistries beyond LFSO. This work establishes a design principle for development of sustainable energy-dense batteries: p-block elements like Sb possess specific orbital-mediated oxygen bonding which contributes to stability of high-valent layered oxide cathodes.

### Paper [18]
Title: Persistent Neutron Irradiation Effects and Partial Recovery in Thin-Film CdS/CdTe Solar Cells
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28472
Authors: Ashraful Mamun (Vince), Andrew Allison (Vince),  Meng-Jen (Vince),  Wang, Etee Kawna Roy, Edward Cazalas, Glenn E. Sjoden, Matthew O. Reese, Joel N. Duenow, Heayoung P. Yoon
Abstract: Thin-film CdTe solar cells are promising candidates for lightweight space power, but device-level studies of their response to neutron irradiation remain scarce. Here, we report the device performance of CdS/CdTe solar cells irradiated for 10 to 150 min in the thermal irradiator of a TRIGA reactor (90 kW), corresponding to nominal total neutron fluences of 3.3 x 10^14 to 5.0 x 10^15 n cm-2. The cells were characterized after 18 months of post-irradiation storage, once the residual activity has decayed to background levels. Co-irradiated FTO-coated glass reference substrates exhibit a wavelength-dependent transmission loss, with the transmittance at 500 nm decreasing from 80 % to 50 % after 60 min of irradiation. The irradiated cells retain rectifying behavior, but the reverse-bias dark current, photocurrent, and efficiency generally decrease with exposure time. Post-irradiation annealing at 70 {\deg}C under one-sun illumination initially improves Jsc and Voc, with the highest efficiency after 4 h. Prolonged annealing, however, causes substantial FF degradation despite continued photocurrent recovery. These trends suggest that irradiation-induced changes in the glass substrate, CdTe absorber, and contacts respond differently to annealing. Understanding underlying defect chemistry and correlating it with device behavior are necessary steps toward radiation-tolerant CdTe photovoltaics for space applications.

### Paper [19]
Title: Curling Superconducting Vortices by Nematic Fluxon-Skyrmion Interaction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28479
Authors: Xiaoqing Bao, Shiyuan Wang, Dekun Shen, Guibin Lan, Tianqi Wang, Yuelin Zhang, Guoqiang Yu, Ka Shen, Haiwen Liu, Jinxing Zhang
Abstract: Superconducting vortex represents a topological entity in type-II superconductors, where the prototypical manifestation of such a macroscopic quantum state holds great promise for applications in future quantum technologies. Deterministic control of the vortex dynamics at the nanoscale is central to enhance superconductors' critical thresholds and design next-generation superconducting quantum devices. However, it is still scientifically challenging due to the intrinsic nature of the vortex pinning landscapes in superconductors. Here, we observe an even-in-field Hall-like signal with about two order of magnitude enhancement in a type-II superconductor/chiral magnet heterostructure, which confines to the field-temperature window where the flux flow coexists with magnetic skyrmions. The polarity of such a giant even-in-field transverse response reverses between orthogonal current directions, suggesting an on-track vortex flow driven by a nematic fluxon-skyrmion interaction analyzed via the extended vortex-viscosity tensor. More critically, a deterministic manipulation of skyrmions at the nanoscale within the hybrid system enables selective and reversible controls over the dissipative behavior and therefore the reconfigurable fluxon transport, mimic the precise control of curling stone in winter Olympic, thereby opening a new avenue towards the exploration of programmable topological and superconducting quantum devices.

### Paper [20]
Title: Interstitial Magnetoelectricity in Altermagnetic Electrides
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28504
Authors: Yue Li, Shibo Fang, Jianhua Wang, Qiang Li, Weizhen Meng, Haiyu Meng, Xiong-Xiong Xue, Jianfeng Wang, Yi Du, Jing Lu, Yee Sin Ang
Abstract: Electrides and altermagnets are two distinct yet intriguing classes of quantum materials, characterized by interstitial anionic electrons and nonrelativistic spin splitting, respectively. The recent prediction of Stoner-instability-driven altermagnetic electride provides a natural bridge between these two fields. Here, we investigate the magnetoelectric response and its related spin-polarized transport in the altermagnetic electride Zr$_2$N monolayer. Using the tight-binding Hubbard model and first-principles calculations, we find that the electric field can break the magnetic compensation between the two interstitial anionic electrons, inducing a finite net magnetization and giving rise to an interstitial electron mediated magnetoelectric response. The magnetoelectric response is further demonstrated through the electrical control of spin conductivity. Our results reveal a magnetoelectric effect in altermagnetic electrides, paving a way towards electride-based spintronic devices.

### Paper [21]
Title: Higher-Order Modulational Instability in Spin-Orbit-Coupled Bose-Einstein Condensates: Finite-Raman Effects and Branch-Resolved Spectral Redistribution
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28514
Authors: Cui-Cui Ding, Boris A. Malomed, Qin Zhou
Abstract: We investigate higher-order modulational instability and the nonlinear spectral redistribution in a spin-orbit-coupled (SOC) binary Bose--Einstein condensate with equal interactions. For finite Raman coupling, the system is nonintegrable and exhibits both nonoscillatory and oscillatory instability regimes. Raman coupling reshapes the instability bands and changes what harmonics can grow. Direct simulations show that this reorganization modifies the subsequent nonlinear sideband dynamics. In the zero-Raman limit, SOC shifts successive harmonics between instability bands and stability gaps at a fixed carrier wavenumber. It thereby controls higher-order instability, distinct breather branches, and frequency-hopping sequences. We derive exact algebraic coefficients for the carrier and arbitrary-order sidebands. This yields an exact geometric power spectrum for the complete infinite sideband sequence and a unified description of carrier depletion, directional imbalance, and sideband decay, including an exact relation between the instability gain and the minimum spectral decay rate. For two coexisting breather branches with the same background and modulation wavenumber, the higher-gain branch can have a smaller maximum carrier depletion, a slower high-order spectral decay, and a weaker directional imbalance. SOC can also restore a symmetric sideband spectrum when the SOC-induced momentum shift compensates the carrier wavenumber, even when both $k$ and $k_L$ are nonzero. These analytical and numerical results clarify how SOC and Raman couplings organize higher-order instability and nonlinear spectral redistribution, providing a unified framework for studying coherent nonlinear-wave dynamics in multicomponent condensates.

### Paper [22]
Title: Ultrahigh Thermal Conductivity and Mirror-Parity Constraints in a Corrugated Two-Dimensional Oxide
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28536
Authors: Babu Ram, Arun S. Nissimagoudar, Weichao Wang, Geunsik Lee
Abstract: Contrary to the expectation of poor heat transport in oxides, first-principles calculations predict an ultrahigh lattice thermal conductivity of 756 $\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ at 300 K in corrugated two-dimensional B$_2$O$_2$. Acoustic phonons dominate heat transport, with the flexural acoustic (ZA) branch contributing 40.62$\%$. Triplet resolved analysis including acoustic and optical partners finds that mirror forbidden three phonon scattering is negligible within numerical precision for sampled ZA modes. These results highlight horizontal mirror symmetry as a constraint on phonon scattering in corrugated oxides.

### Paper [23]
Title: Tritium speciation and transport in molten FLiBe: parameters, uncertainties and validation priorities
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28577
Authors: Joe Lunn, Teresa Orr, Megan Leyland, Ayobami Daramola, R\'emi Delaporte-Mathurin, James Dark, Graeme J. Ackland, Prashanth Srinivasan, Andrew P. Horsfield, Mikhail Yu. Lavrentiev, Duc Nguyen-Manh, Mark R. Gilbert, Matthew R. Ryder
Abstract: Molten lithium fluoride-beryllium fluoride (FLiBe) is a candidate liquid breeder material for fusion applications, but predictive modelling of tritium retention, transport and extraction depends on chemical and interfacial assumptions that remain only partly resolved. Tritium produced in lithium-bearing fluoride salts may occur in oxidised or fluoride-associated states and can exchange into molecular hydrogen-isotope species such as HT. The relative populations and apparent transport behaviour depend on salt composition and condition, redox state, and interactions at gas-liquid and salt-wall boundaries. This review assesses the evidence for tritium speciation and transport in molten FLiBe, with emphasis on the solubility, effective diffusivity, permeability, exchange and boundary-transfer parameters required by continuum models. The available evidence motivates a two-pool continuum description with separate oxidised or fluoride-associated and molecular-hydrogen pools, without assigning a unique microscopic species. This description applies where states omitted from these pools do not materially affect tritium inventory or transport in the salt. We distinguish directly measured quantities from fitted or inferred effective parameters, recommend how source-specific transport data should be reported and compared, and identify the measurements required to constrain the remaining uncertainties in speciation, exchange, bulk transport and boundary transfer.

### Paper [24]
Title: Competing magnetic correlations in the square-lattice Hubbard model with spin-dependent nearest-neighbor and next-nearest-neighbor hopping
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28593
Authors: Rana Imran Mushtaq, Ji Liu, Yinlong Li, Wing Chi Yu, Xiaosen Yang, Ho-Kin Tang
Abstract: Altermagnets have spin-split bands without net magnetization. The splitting can come from a spin-dependent nearest-neighbor (NN) hopping anisotropy $t_A$ or from a spin-dependent next-nearest-neighbor (NNN) hopping $t'$. Both change sign under a $90^\circ$ rotation and are present together when the lattice has no mirror planes, yet they have so far been studied only separately. Here we study the two-dimensional square-lattice Hubbard model with both $t_A$ and $t'$, using constrained-path quantum Monte Carlo at $U=4t$ and scanning the electron filling $n$, $t_A$ and $t'$. We find four types of magnetic correlations: N\'eel, stripe, diagonal, and spiral spin-density-wave. The phase diagram depends on both hoppings together. Diagonal correlations require finite $t_A$ and are suppressed by $t'$. Stripe correlations appear only once $t'$ is large enough to overcome the diagonal tendency set by $t_A$. N\'eel correlations survive at large $t_A$ and large $t'$, where the NNN model alone gives stripe or spiral correlations. The noninteracting susceptibility matches the dominant wavevector at small $t'$, but not the N\'eel correlations at $t'\geq0.3$, which come from the interaction. Finite-size scaling with three lattice sizes is consistent with long-range order only near half filling; at the stripe, spiral and diagonal points we tested, the structure factor per site extrapolates to zero. The joint behavior persists up to $U=6t$.

### Paper [25]
Title: Hartree--Fock analysis of the bilayer Hubbard model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28597
Authors: E. Langmann, J. Lenells
Abstract: We study the half-filled Hubbard model on two parallel square lattices with on-site repulsion $U>0$ and with nearest-neighbor hopping specified by a constant $t>0$ within each plane and by another constant $t_\perp \geq 0$ between the planes. Working in the Hartree--Fock approximation, we obtain asymptotic formulas for the N\'eel temperature and the antiferromagnetic gap ratio in the weak coupling limit. We also determine the behavior of the antiferromagnetic gap ratio near the critical curve separating the paramagnetic and antiferromagnetic regions in the phase diagram. Furthermore, we present numerical phase diagrams away from half-filling, and we make an analytical conjecture regarding their structure.

### Paper [26]
Title: A High-Frequency Flux-Driven DC-SQUID Threshold Detector at the Single-Microwave-Photon Energy Scale: A Numerical Feasibility Study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28627
Authors: M. Y. Wang, Y. Q. Chai, S. N. Wang, L. F. Wei
Abstract: In our recent work (Phys. Rev. B \textbf{113}, 224503 (2026)), we showed numerically that rapidly sweeping the bias current of a current-biased Josephson junction threshold detector (CBJJ-JTD) drives it out of equilibrium. This substantially reduces the effect of thermal noise on microwave detection and may enable threshold detection near the energy of a single microwave photon. The scheme requires very low damping ($\beta \leq 10^{-4}$) and a very fast bias-current sweep ($v/\beta \geq 1$), both of which are difficult to achieve with available junctions and low-pass-filtered cryogenic measurement lines. Here we propose a more experimentally accessible nonequilibrium threshold detector based on a high-frequency flux-driven direct-current superconducting quantum interference device (DC-SQUID) and investigate it by numerically solving the resistively and capacitively shunted junction (RCSJ) equations. The applied flux drives a circulating supercurrent and sustains nonequilibrium phase dynamics throughout the bias-current sweep, removing the need for a fast sweep or for transient oscillations set by the initial phase. Using typical experimental device parameters, we calculate switching-current distributions (SCDs) with and without a microwave input for both driven and undriven SQUIDs. We quantify detection performance using receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curves and their area under the curve (AUC). The simulations show that high-frequency flux drive reduces the impact of thermal noise and enables statistical discrimination of weak microwave pulses at the single-photon energy scale. We also numerically estimate detector characteristics such as phase selectivity and dynamic range.

### Paper [27]
Title: Spherical HgTe nanocrystal: a multi-band effective mass study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28689
Authors: Rui Li
Abstract: The size-quantized energies, eigenstates, and optical selection rules in a spherical HgTe nanocrystal are studied by solving the multi-band effective-mass equations analytically. The six-band Kane model, involving both the $\Gamma_{6}$ and $\Gamma_{8}$ bands within the spherical approximation, is employed in the derivation. The eigenstates can be classified by both the total angular momentum and the parity. As the nanocrystal radius varies, crossings occur not only between electron and hole energy levels but also among the hole levels themselves. The lowest two electron states (in each spin subspace) are surface states, one with even parity and the other with odd parity. The optical selection rules consist of two parts, namely, the parity selection and the angular momentum selection.

### Paper [28]
Title: Corrections to the IPR in the localized phase of the Anderson model on the Bethe lattice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28705
Authors: Tommaso Rizzo, Marco Tarzia, Angelica Vecchiarelli
Abstract: We show that finite-size effects in the localized phase of the Anderson model on the Bethe lattice are highly anomalous. On random regular graphs of $N$ sites the inverse participation ratio (IPR) approaches its thermodynamic-limit value as $I_2(N)-I_2^\infty\sim N^{-\kappa(W)}$, with a disorder-dependent exponent $0<\kappa<1$ throughout the localized phase, and the same exponent governs the correction due to a finite imaginary regulator, $I_2(\eta)-I_2^\infty\sim\eta^{\kappa(W)}$. We derive $\kappa = 2\beta-1$, with $\beta$ the power-law tail exponent of the local density of states distribution, in a solvable model sharing the criticality of the Anderson model on the Bethe lattice. We then show that the same mechanism operates in the Anderson model itself, and find quantitative agreement with the numerics. A self-consistent one-loop treatment of the loops of finite graphs independently reproduces the finite-size corrections. Physically, a spectral identity and an exact sum rule tie both corrections to rare Mott-like resonances hybridizing distant localized eigenstates: a finite graph lacks the resonant pairs whose centers would lie farther apart than its size allows. Our results identify the scaling variable needed to extrapolate IPR data to the thermodynamic limit.

### Paper [29]
Title: Anomalous corrections to the inverse participation ratio in the localized phase on the Bethe lattice: insights from a solvable "toy" non-linear $\sigma$-model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28709
Authors: Tommaso Rizzo, Marco Tarzia, Angelica Vecchiarelli
Abstract: In the localized phase of the Anderson model on the Bethe lattice, the inverse participation ratio (IPR) approaches its asymptotic value as the imaginary regulator $\eta \to 0$ with an anomalous power law, $I_2(\eta) - I_2^\infty \propto \eta^{2\beta-1}$, where $\beta \in (1/2,1)$ is the tail exponent of the local density of states distribution. We derive it analytically in a "toy" non-linear $\sigma$-model on the hyperbolic superplane, which shares the exotic criticality of the Bethe-lattice Anderson transition. Matching the bulk solution of its cavity equation to a boundary layer set by the symmetry-breaking field $H$ gives a correction $\propto H^{2\beta-1}$, controlled by the subdominant root $1-\beta$ of the linearized equation; at criticality it becomes $\propto 1/|\ln H|$. Under a few natural assumptions the same mechanism operates in the Anderson model, where the correction is a zero mode of the $\eta=0$ cavity recursion, as we confirm numerically, also for finite-size corrections. Through a spectral identity and an exact sum rule, the same exponent fixes exactly the decay $\omega^{-\mu}$, with $\mu = 2(1-\beta)$, of the correlation function of two eigenstates at energy distance $\omega$, including its amplitude, as we confirm by exact diagonalization, population dynamics on the infinite lattice, and analytically in the toy model. A counting of Mott resonances between distant localized eigenstates explains this physically; on finite graphs, finite-size corrections reflect a deficit of doubly-peaked eigenstates. Finally, we derive the finite-size scaling of the IPR in the variable $(2\beta-1)\ln N$, which describes both finite-$\eta$ and finite-$N$ data. It yields a Kosterlitz-type renormalization-group flow of the running fractal dimension: finite fractal dimensions of localized states at accessible sizes are a logarithmic finite-size effect.

### Paper [30]
Title: Minimal Three-Band Model and $d$-Wave Superconductivity in La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_8$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28747
Authors: Ao Zhang, Chao Deng, Wenfeng Wu, Run Yang, Zhixiang Shi, Liang Si
Abstract: Motivated by the recent observation of ambient-pressure superconductivity in Pr- and Y-substituted La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_8$, we combined density-functional theory (DFT), dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT), random phase approximation (RPA), and fluctuation exchange approximation (FLEX) methods to study its electronic structure and superconducting pairing symmetry. DFT band analysis indicates that the low-energy states are mainly dominated by three Ni-3$d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbitals with weak $d$-$p$ hybridization. Multi-orbital DMFT calculations yield an average $d_{x^2-y^2}$ filling of about 0.87, close to the optimal value for infinite-layer nickelates. By comparing the DMFT results of $d_{x^2-y^2}$ only and full Ni-3$d$ models, we find that their electronic properties are almost completely consistent, demonstrating the effectiveness of the simplified $d_{x^2-y^2}$ model. RPA and FLEX calculations based on this model consistently indicate a dominant $d_{x^2-y^2}$-wave pairing instability. These results establish La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_8$ as a system whose electronic structure closely resembles that of hole-doped cuprates, suggesting that the electronic pockets near the $\Gamma$- and $A$-points, which are characteristic of infinite-layer nickelates, may not be essential for superconductivity. The calculated superconducting transition temperature ($T_C$) is approximately 10\,K, in good agreement with experimental observations.

### Paper [31]
Title: Quantum Hall Effect in a 3D Cross-Dimensional Quantum Anomalous Hall System
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28751
Authors: Liang-Liang Cheng, Ao-Long Li, Zhi-Qiang Zhang, Hua Jiang
Abstract: We systematically investigate cross-dimensional transport and the resulting quantum Hall effect in 3D quantum anomalous Hall effect systems, which are constructed by coupling two Weyl-semimetal Hamiltonians. Our study unveils that the system hosts highly anisotropic surface states and chiral modes, leading to rich quantum Hall responses. Crucially, we clarify how magnetic fields applied along three high-symmetry directions dictate the evolution of the Chern numbers, chiral modes, and Hall responses. We discover that chiral modes along different directions can originate from distinct mechanisms, giving rise to various magnetic modulation characteristics. Furthermore, we unveil the breakdown of the conventional Chern-number-based bulk-boundary correspondence under magnetic fields, where the Chern numbers alone fail to fully characterize the evolution of the chiral states. This anomalous behavior is attributed to the unique momentum-space distributions of surface bands. These insights are further validated numerically through multi-terminal Hall transport simulations. Our findings expand the understanding of magnetic-field-driven mechanisms in 3D topological systems and provide deeper insights into the interplay between quantized Hall effects and Chern numbers in 3D systems.

### Paper [32]
Title: Synthesis Method-dependent Resist-like Behavior of Liquid Crystalline Graphene Oxide Films Under Electron Beam Irradiation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28754
Authors: A. Sahakyan, Ghada E. Khedr, A. A. Hovhannisyan, G. Gevorgyan, K. Margaryan, G. Shakhatuni, M. Aleksanyan, Mohamed Aly Saad Aly, N. Nwaji, H. Gharagulyan
Abstract: Here, the electron-beam-induced structural evolution and patterning behavior of chitosan-functionalized GO films, specifically those using chemically vs electrochemically exfoliated GO, are investigated. Experimental results (supported by DFT calculations) revealed that electron irradiation drives controllable microstructure formation in GO by selectively triggering ablation and densification pathways, which are dictated by the GOs synthesis route and initial oxidation state. Furthermore, a comprehensive characterization study was conducted to compare the optostructural properties of the present system prior and subsequent to electron-beam irradiation. Moreover, a systematic study of the films mechanical properties across both micro- and macroscopic scales was also performed to elucidate the underlying mechanisms responsible for the observed phenomena. The results demonstrate that the integration of mask-free electron-beam patterning, multiscale characterization, and DFT modeling establishes a previously unexplored synthesis-structure-irradiation relationship in GO films, providing fundamental insights into irradiation-induced microstructure evolution and opening new avenues for the design of GO-based micro- and nanostructured materials.

### Paper [33]
Title: Local Pressure Induced Quantum Dot to Ring Transition and Aharonov Bohm Effect in Reconfigurable MoTe2 Bilayers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28786
Authors: Ibsal Assi, A. Belayadi, Hocine Bahlouli, Udo Schwingenschl\"ogl, J. P. F. LeBlanc
Abstract: We demonstrate that local pressure provides an effective means of engineering reconfigurable quantum confinement in MoTe2 homobilayers. By applying a smooth, spatially varying pressure profile, we show that the confinement geometry can be continuously tuned from a conventional quantum dot to a quantum ring, with the emergence of annular confinement being a distinctive feature of AB-stacked bilayers. This crossover originates from the pressure-induced spatial modulation in the electronic parameters, which produces an off-center minimum in the effective confinement potential, thereby redistributing the ground-state probability density away from the center and giving rise to an annular confinement geometry. In the quantum-ring regime, a perpendicular magnetic field gives rise to a flux-periodic energy spectrum and persistent currents, providing characteristic signatures of the Aharonov-Bohm effect. By coupling the pressure-induced quantum ring to source and drain leads, we further demonstrate pronounced Aharonov-Bohm conductance oscillations, accompanied by real-space current patterns exhibiting constructive and destructive interference at integer and half-integer magnetic flux quanta, respectively. These findings establish locally pressurized MoTe2 bilayers as a lithography-free platform for reconfigurable quantum confinement and realizing pressure-tunable Aharonov-Bohm interferometry in two dimensional materials.

### Paper [34]
Title: Floquet-Induced Valley Polarization and Topological Phases in Twisted Bilayer Graphene Heterostructures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28797
Authors: A. Belayadi, I. A. Assi, A. Johansson, C. I. Osuala, H. Bahlouli, Udo Schwingenschl\"ogl, J. P. F. LeBlanc, A. Abbout
Abstract: We theoretically investigate twisted bilayer graphene (tTBG) heterostructures subjected to circularly polarized light. Inversion symmetry is broken through the combined application of an out-of-plane electric field and the incorporation of insulating substrates, enabling precise control of the valley degrees of freedom and associated topological phases. We identify a regime in which the valley degeneracy is lifted: one valley retains a gapless, linear Dirac-like dispersion, whereas the other develops a finite band gap and becomes electronically inactive. The interplay between Floquet driving and inversion-symmetry breaking induces topological phase transitions characterized by Chern numbers C = -2, C = -1, and C = 0, together with the emergence of perfectly valley-polarized domain-wall channels. Furthermore, magnetic asymmetry arising from proximity to adjacent two-dimensional ferromagnetic contacts enables spin-valley filtering. These findings establish the potential of graphene-based heterostructures as highly tunable systems for controlling spin and valley degrees of freedom.

### Paper [35]
Title: Learning free energy across a differentiable temperature range by deep generative modeling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28810
Authors: Yaowen Zhang, Chen Chen, Wanyu Lin, Shuo-Hui Li, Ding Pan
Abstract: Free energy governs the directionality, stability, and temperature sensitivity of physical, chemical, and biological transformations. Conventional free energy calculations, such as thermodynamic integration and free energy perturbation, often have to be performed at individual temperatures and are limited by the high cost of sampling an extensive set of thermodynamic states. Here, we introduce learned temperature-differentiable free energy perturbation (TD-FEP), a deep generative framework that learns a temperature-conditioned, invertible mapping between reference and target systems through data-free, energy-based optimization. TD-FEP thereby unifies one-shot state generation and free energy calculation across a continuous temperature range. We validated TD-FEP across a broad range of applications, including isolated molecules, periodic materials, and chemical reactions.

### Paper [36]
Title: Large Deviations and Critical Scaling in the Finite-Population Granovetter Threshold Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28811
Authors: Jos\'e F. Fontanari
Abstract: We develop a systematic asymptotic analysis of the exact finite-population solution of Granovetter's threshold model, in which a single initial instigator triggers a cascade among agents with independently distributed adoption thresholds. By expressing the propagation probability as an order-statistic boundary-crossing event, we formulate its macroscopic large-deviation rate as a constrained relative-entropy minimization problem. For Beta-distributed thresholds, we obtain explicit solutions for all shape parameters $\alpha$ and $\beta$, identifying four possible optimal empirical threshold profiles: fully free, fully pinned, pinned--free, and free--pinned. These profiles determine both the exponential probability of macroscopic cascades and the threshold configurations responsible for their occurrence. Importance-sampling estimates confirm the analytical rate functions, while sequential Monte Carlo simulations independently reproduce the predicted optimal profiles. We also revisit the critical scaling near $\alpha_c=1$ and confirm the principal finite-size scaling laws: the mean active fraction decays as $N^{-1/2}$ for uniform thresholds and as $N^{-1}$ for $\alpha=1$ and $0<\beta<1$. In the latter regime, we further derive an explicit scaling function for the logarithmic critical window from the microscopic branching-process limit. Our results provide a general framework for determining the probabilities and characteristic configurations of rare collective events in threshold-driven cascade processes

### Paper [37]
Title: Statistical mechanics of linear classification on hierarchical tree data
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28824
Authors: Jerome Garnier-Brun, Federica Kulka, Carlo Lucibello
Abstract: Deep networks owe much of their success to their ability to exploit the structure in data, from the grammar of language to the multi-scale organization of images. Yet, many statistical mechanics approaches to structured data rely on Gaussian equivalence, whose assumptions can fail when a few latent variables influence all input coordinates. In this work, we take a step beyond this setting with a simple setup, in which a linear classifier is trained to infer the root of the classical broadcast process on a tree from its $N$ leaves. Despite this simplicity, the data already display non-Gaussian statistics along the direction carrying the label, as well as strong finite-size effects. By retaining the exact law along this direction and approximating the transverse fluctuations as Gaussian, we obtain a finite-depth replica theory that accurately describes the measured learning curves. At large depth and sufficiently weak regularization, we uncover a double descent whose two stages are separated in scale. In a range of broadcast noise below the reconstruction threshold, the first descent occurs at a number of samples scaling as $N^\omega$, with $0<\omega<1$ set by the data statistics, whereas the overfitting peak occurs at order $N$ samples. Beyond linear classifiers, this setup could serve as a controlled starting point to study deeper architectures and generative models on hierarchical data.

### Paper [38]
Title: Thickness-Dependent Morphology and Electrical Transport in Ultrathin Amorphous MoSi Films
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28862
Authors: O. O. Leha, O. V. Zraichenko, S. I. Kryvonohov, V. Yu. Lyakhno
Abstract: We investigate the thickness-dependent surface morphology and electrical transport of ultrathin amorphous Mo50Si50 films deposited by magnetron co-sputtering. Atomic force microscopy of Si-capped heterostructures on Si/SiO2 reveals smooth surfaces throughout the investigated thickness range, with RMS roughness below 0.2 nm and a non-monotonic thickness dependence characterized by a minimum at 1.92 nm. Room-temperature sheet resistance follows an approximately inverse-thickness dependence for films with nominal thicknesses of about 1.9-5 nm, whereas a pronounced deviation from this scaling develops below approximately 1.5-2 nm. Temperature-dependent measurements further reveal a superconducting transition in the 30-nm film, while the 1.54-nm film shows a strong resistance increase upon cooling clearly indicates a superconductor-insulator transition. In the 2-20 K range, the sheet conductance of the 1.54-nm film exhibits a reproducible logarithmic temperature dependence, Gs(T)=G0+Bln(T/T0), consistent with quantum corrections to diffusive transport in a strongly disordered quasi-two-dimensional conductor. These results identify a thickness-sensitive transport regime in ultrathin MoSi while demonstrating that the corresponding heterostructures retain comparatively smooth surface morphology.

### Paper [39]
Title: Time-Reversed Task Events and Thermodynamic Bounds for Stochastic Thermal Machines
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28874
Authors: Kaiyuan Cao, Bei Xiao, Jian Wang
Abstract: We develop a task-based framework that relates finite-time performance to thermodynamic irreversibility in stochastic heat engines and refrigerators. For tasks defined by reaching an accumulated-work threshold within a prescribed time, we construct the reverse event through exact trajectory time reversal. The binary relative entropy between forward and reverse task probabilities provides a lower bound on mean entropy production. Combined with the first law and entropy balance, this bound constrains irreversible work loss in engines and excess input work in refrigerators. We illustrate the framework with a three-level stochastic system and test the bounds against its full dynamics. These results show how success-or-failure statistics alone can reveal irreversibility and constrain the energetic costs of finite-time thermal tasks. We also outline an experimental route to testing the framework using a monitored three-level system coupled to engineered thermal reservoirs and a work channel.

### Paper [40]
Title: Charge-fluctuation hot lines and superconductivity in Ba$_{1-x}$K$_x$BiO$_3$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28879
Authors: Xiangang Wan, Sergey Y. Savrasov
Abstract: The origin of superconductivity near 30~K in Ba$_{0.6}$K$_{0.4}$BiO$_3$ has remained a long-standing puzzle. Tunneling experiments give an electron--boson coupling $\lambda\approx 0.7$--1.2, whereas density-functional calculations give $\lambda\approx 0.3$, concentrated in the breathing mode near 50--70~meV. We propose that the missing coupling of about 0.7 comes from dynamical charge fluctuations at Q=(pi,pi,pi)/a, the ordering vector of the charge-disproportionated parent compound, and introduce an overdamped boson model to describe them. Using a tight-binding fit to the conduction band found from density-functional calculations at $x=0.4$, we show that such a boson scatters electrons strongly only on hot lines of the Fermi surface, small loops around the $\langle 111\rangle$ directions where k and k+Q are both at the Fermi level. We derive closed-form self-energies, quasiparticle residues and band reconstructions, including shadow bands and hot-line pseudogaps for long-range correlations, and show that a sum rule fixes the Fermi-surface-averaged coupling independently of the boson frequency and correlation length. Combining the LDA phonon with an overdamped mode of frequency $\omega_m\approx 25$~meV and coupling $\lambda\approx 0.7$, anisotropic Eliashberg calculations reproduce $T_c\approx 25$--30~K for correlation lengths of two to three lattice spacings and predict a gap enhanced by a factor of 3--4 on the hot loops. For longer correlation lengths an $s$-wave state competes closely with a sign-changing $T_{2g}$ state confined to the hot loops. In the random-phase approximation for an extended Hubbard model, the boson emerges naturally near a charge instability at $R$, but purely electronic fluctuations are too weak and too long-ranged; coupling to the breathing-mode lattice distortion is one possible way to strengthen them.

### Paper [41]
Title: Tuning of superconducting and physical properties of Mo$_{1-x}$Si$_x$ thin films for Superconducting Nanowire Single-Photon Detector Applications
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28927
Authors: Oleh V. Zraichenko, Oleksandr O. Leha, Sergei V. Bengus, Valerii Yu. Lyakhno
Abstract: We investigate the normal-state electrical properties of ultrathin amorphous Mo$_{1-x}$Si$_x$ films deposited by DC magnetron co-sputtering over a broad range of compositions and thicknesses. Films with Si contents of $x = 0.14$ to $0.5$ were deposited on Si/SiO$_2$ and Si/Si$_3$N$_4$ substrates (monocrystalline Si with SiO$_2$ and S$_3$N$_4$ buffer layers respectfully); X-ray diffraction confirms an amorphous structure across the full composition range, while X-ray reflectometry is used to verify film thicknesses. The room-temperature sheet resistance ranges from 110 to 1990 $\Omega/\square$, corresponding to resistivities of 125-478 $\mu\Omega\cdot$cm. The sheet resistance generally increases with increasing Si content and decreasing film thickness, although the coupled variation of composition and thickness limits the quantitative separation of these effects. Additional measurements on thicker reference films show superconducting transition temperatures between 5.2 and 7.7 K, with $T_c$ decreasing monotonically at higher Si contents. A comparison of films on different substrate types shows no systematic difference between Si/SiO$_2$ and Si/Si$_3$N$_4$, while films on Al$_2$O$_3$ exhibit elevated sheet resistance at the lower thicknesses. These results characterize the accessible parameter space of our deposition process and provide a basis for the selection of film stoichiometry for detector fabrication.

### Paper [42]
Title: Observation of nonadiabatic quantum tunneling in a driven correlated insulator
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28980
Authors: Mingyao Guo, Radu Andrei, Jaena Park, Je-Geun Park, Eugene Demler, David Hsieh
Abstract: Many emerging solid-state platforms for Floquet engineering, nonlinear transport, and high harmonic generation operate near the Keldysh crossover, where optical fields are sufficiently strong and fast to reshape electronic states on sub-cycle timescales. Despite its importance, the microscopic pathways for photocarrier generation in this regime remain poorly understood, as neither perturbative multiphoton absorption nor adiabatic tunneling provides an adequate description. Here, we perform time-resolved optical spectroscopy on the correlated van der Waals insulator NiPS$_3$ while systematically tuning the drive electric field and frequency over a wide range. In the crossover regime, we observe multiphoton-like resonances that are blue-shifted by ponderomotive effects within an otherwise tunneling-dominated absorption channel - a clear spectroscopic signature of adiabaticity breakdown. Hubbard-model simulations based on a time-dependent Gaussian variational approach qualitatively reproduce these features. Together, our results elucidate the microscopic mechanisms of charge excitation in the Keldysh crossover regime and identify a key ingredient for controlling strong-field nonlinear dynamics in correlated materials.

### Paper [43]
Title: Finite-size reversal of a multiparticle correction to local spin spectra in a flux-disordered Kitaev honeycomb model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29070
Authors: David C. Ronquillo
Abstract: A spin flip in the Kitaev honeycomb model changes the flux background and therefore the Hamiltonian seen by the matter fermions. Can a one-particle approximation with the correct total spin intensity nevertheless misestimate the depth of a depression in the local spin spectrum? We test this question in a frozen, flux-disordered ensemble by retaining the actual one-particle transition energies and relative spectral weights after the spin flip, then rescaling those weights to match the full intensity. We measure the depression by subtracting the central intensity from the average at two flanking frequencies. For spin-created states with odd final quasiparticle number and nonzero one-particle weight, restoring multiparticle weight shallows the depression on 24 sites and deepens it on both 1,536-site shapes and the 3,456- and 6,144-site tori, as judged by the median paired correction at each of two energy resolutions. For these states, the one-particle approximation overestimates the mean dip depth by about 16-18% on 24 sites and underestimates it by 33-56% in the 6,144-site sample. Extrapolating from 24 sites would therefore give the wrong sign for this correction on those four larger tori under the same disorder prescription.

### Paper [44]
Title: Interface invariants of rhombohedral graphene with layer-dependent potentials and couplings
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29093
Authors: Yuelong Li
Abstract: Domain walls in the perpendicular bias of rhombohedral (ABC-stacked) graphene bind valley-resolved interface modes whose signed number is fixed by a bulk-difference invariant. We determine which values of this invariant are reachable when the layer potentials are arbitrary monotone profiles, as produced by screening, and when the interlayer couplings vary from bond to bond. Within the single-particle continuum model, the invariant of an $m$-layer stack equals the difference of occupied angular weights of the zero-momentum levels, which reduces the classification to the ordering of those levels. At equal coupling, nonlinear monotone potentials reproduce exactly the set of invariants of linear potentials, also under the requirement of a common energy gap: screening moves phase boundaries but creates no new invariant values. With bond-dependent coupling, we characterize all occupied configurations. The range of attainable invariants is unchanged, but the gaps inside it are filled, beginning at five layers. Reflection-symmetric stacks realize a large subfamily with a guaranteed common gap, and lower bounds on single-bulk gaps complete the set of invariants with a common global gap for five and six layers. Among the examples is an eight-layer domain wall defined purely by gates, whose number of co-propagating channels per valley switches from 8 to 16 as the gate strength increases. Exact transfer-matrix calculations of the interface spectrum reproduce every predicted signed crossing count. A norm bound gives the minimum spread of interlayer coupling compatible with a prescribed gap.

### Paper [45]
Title: A Fiber-Optic Platform for Cryogenic Laser-Pump Neutron-Probe Magneto-Optical Measurements
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29114
Authors: Lazar L. Kish, Andrei T. Savici, Tyler K. White, Vlad N. Sedov, Andrey A. Podlesnyak, Matthias D. Frontzek, Barry L. Winn, Igor A. Zaliznyak
Abstract: We report a fiber-optic laser-pump neutron-probe method for momentum- and frequency-resolved magneto-optical measurements. The technique uses inelastic neutron scattering to measure time-dependent two-point magnetization correlations, S(Q,$\omega$), after resonant photoexcitation at cryogenic temperatures. We demonstrate it by measuring nonequilibrium thermalization dynamics in Yb-based quantum magnets.

### Paper [46]
Title: DeepDOSReasoner: Physics-Grounded Reasoning for Density-of-States Prediction and Materials Discovery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29132
Authors: Yingheng Wang, Francesco Ricci, Zhilong Wang, Tao Yu, Junwen Bai, Xiangyun Lei, Shufeng Kong, Fengqi You, John M. Gregoire, Carla P. Gomes
Abstract: Predicting a material's behavior from its atomic structure is a longstanding goal of the physical sciences. The electronic and phonon densities of states (eDOS and phDOS) govern key electronic, optical, vibrational, and thermodynamic properties, yet their calculation using density functional theory (DFT) is costly: about 60% of Materials Project entries lack eDOS and over 80% lack phDOS. Existing crystal-to-spectrum models rely on pooled structural representations and pointwise regression, which blur spectral features and violate total-state conservation. Here we introduce DeepDOSReasoner, a physics-grounded framework that decodes spectra through atom-to-energy source attention and refines the normalized DOS through learned continuity-equation transport, enforcing nonnegativity and total-state conservation by construction. DeepDOSReasoner achieves the highest prediction accuracy across all evaluated metrics for both eDOS and phDOS, recovers element-resolved contributions without supervision, and most reliably identifies rare valence-band (VB) gaps. In a prospective screen of 8,106 uncharted materials validated by new DFT calculations, its predictions remain the most accurate among the methods evaluated, with 25 of its top 50 candidates confirmed as VB-gap materials. Three case studies spanning substituted compounds, photovoltaic chalcogenides, and a high-entropy alloy demonstrate transfer across materials classes. Ablations confirm that both stages are necessary, while the model retains comparable accuracy when trained on only 25% of the data. Predicting spectra in milliseconds enables exhaustive screening; we release predictions filling the missing eDOS for 91,405 and phDOS for 127,768 Materials Project entries. DeepDOSReasoner provides a blueprint for integrating machine learning with physical reasoning toward high-throughput spectral screening and autonomous materials discovery.

### Paper [47]
Title: An informational interpretation of the reverse nonequilibrium molecular dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29174
Authors: Yuxing Jiao, Mingcheng Yang
Abstract: Reverse nonequilibrium molecular dynamics (RNEMD) is an efficient simulation method for determining transport coefficients. In this method, a flux is imposed by a seemingly artificial nonlocal exchange operation and the corresponding thermodynamic gradient is induced as a result, apparently reversing the usual force-flux causality. Although this method is well studied as a simulation technique, developing an information-thermodynamic interpretation of its Maxwell-demon-type operation and the associated apparent reversal of force-flux causality would provide a solid physical foundation for algorithms of this type. Here, we develop an information-reservoir interpretation for a representative RNEMD scheme for determining self-diffusivity. We show that the statistical bias of the information reservoir defines an information affinity that sustains a concentration gradient and a diffusive flux in the steady state. We further derive the linear transport coefficient corresponding to the information affinity in the linear response regime. Using the large deviation functional for the steady-state densities and currents, we also establish a fluctuation theorem for entropy production and the thermodynamic uncertainty relation linking entropy production to the fluctuations of the particle current. Computer simulations are performed to corroborate our theoretical results.

### Paper [48]
Title: CLFF: Automated Framework for Concurrent Learning Force Fields and Material Property Calculations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29187
Authors: Cao Thang Nguyen, Sung Youb Kim
Abstract: Machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) offer an efficient paradigm for large-scale atomistic simulations with near first-principles accuracy. However, developing and deploying these potentials often involves intricate procedures that require substantial manual effort and domain expertise. In this work, we present CLFF, an open-source Python framework designed to automate concurrent learning workflows for MLIPs. CLFF integrates model training, MLIP-driven sampling, uncertainty-based candidate selection, quantum-mechanical labeling, and dataset augmentation into a unified workflow. The framework currently supports several modern graph-based MLIP architectures and is compatible with widely used computational chemistry and materials science tools. A distinguishing feature of CLFF is its ability to asynchronously distribute computational tasks across multiple heterogeneous remote computing resources, enabling users to aggregate fragmented hardware infrastructure within a single controllable workflow. In addition to concurrent learning, CLFF provides automated workflows for initial data generation and material property calculations, including phonon dispersion and potential energy surface scans. By minimizing manual intervention and enhancing reproducibility, CLFF offers a flexible platform for developing and deploying MLIPs in computational materials research.

### Paper [49]
Title: AgenticMM: A Material-Centered Multi-Agent Architecture for Scalable First-Principles Materials Modeling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29248
Authors: Zhenkun Yuan, Geoffroy Hautier
Abstract: Multi-agent frameworks are attracting increasing attention as a means of autonomously driving complex, long-running workflows in first-principles materials modeling. However, such frameworks commonly organize AI agents by computational task or scientific role. When scaled across many materials, this organization can require repeated transfers of material-specific context and complicate persistent execution and resource coordination. Here, we introduce AgenticMM, a material-centered multi-agent architecture in which each material has a persistent agent that owns its complete workflow and retains its scientific context, while a persistent coordinator allocates shared resources, mediates communication, and tracks campaign progress. AgenticMM is designed to incorporate reusable, prompt-based skills as modular definitions of these workflows. Using DefectSkills prompts, we demonstrate the feasibility of AgenticMM through a multistage point-defect campaign in which material-specific agents concurrently perform host electronic-structure, phase-stability, and intrinsic-defect calculations for five chemically diverse semiconductors.

### Paper [50]
Title: Quantum-Metric-Driven Hollow Wigner Crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29317
Authors: Zekun Zhuang
Abstract: Recent studies have revealed the profound influence of Berry curvature on electron crystallization, yet the independent role of the quantum metric remains unclear. Here, we introduce a minimal three-band continuum model with separately tunable quantum metric and Berry flux. Employing the Hartree-Fock approach, we find that a conventional s-wave Wigner crystal can develop a hollow charge distribution with depleted density at lattice sites, without a change in symmetry or topology. We show that this density reconstruction occurs even at vanishing Berry curvature and arises from strong quantum metric and the nontrivial angular structure of the form factor. In our model, the continuous crossover between hollow and conventional WCs is accompanied by anomalous direct exchange interactions, nearly vanishing at intermediate quantum metric but enhanced by over an order of magnitude in the strong-metric regime. These results reveal how quantum metric can reshape both the charge distribution and exchange interactions of electron crystals beyond simple wave-function delocalization.

### Paper [51]
Title: Graph Neural Networks for Accurate Quasiparticle Energy Levels in Large Supercells of Transition Metal Dichalcogenides
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29349
Authors: Rafael R. Del Grande, David A. Strubbe
Abstract: The GW method is the one of the main workhorses of the materials science community to accurately calculate the electronic bandgap of materials, as it overcomes the gap underestimation problem from Density Functional Theory (DFT). Despite its accuracy, its computational cost becomes prohibitive when calculations have more than 10-100 atoms, which makes more difficult its use for defects, surfaces and moir\'e systems that demand large supercells. To overcome this problem, we developed a Graph Neural Network trained with smaller GW calculations which can make accurate predictions on large supercells at a small fraction of the computational cost.
  Our model uses as features the projection of DFT wavefunctions into localized atomic orbitals, while graph nodes represent the atomic positions and edges represent atom to atom distances, which captures the system geometry. We explore how to do "small AI", providing a workflow of how to create a personal and convenient dataset of GW calculations to train the model that can be applied to different materials so that the final user does not need to rely on massive datasets. We define what must be included in the training dataset to capture the physics of our objective simulation, and perform a Bayesian optimization of hyperparameters. In the case of monolayer MoS$_2$ with a sulfur vacancy our GNN models achieve high accuracy (MAE ranging 10 to 50 meV) with a simplified workflow applicable to other materials, accelerating accurate large electronic structure calculations.

### Paper [52]
Title: Collineations of particles as a tool to study nucleation and crystallization processes in liquids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29360
Authors: V. A. Levashov
Abstract: We suggest several methods that can be used for identification of crystal nuclei in the studies of the nucleation and crystallization processes in multicomponent systems and in situations when the forming crystalline phase is different from the FCC, HCP or BCC lattices. We suggest several methods that can be used for identification of crystal nuclei in the studies of the nucleation and crystallization processes in multicomponent systems and in situations when the forming crystalline phase is different from the FCC, HCP or BCC lattices. The approaches are based on consideration of collineations of particles. They allow finding nuclei and separating them from each other. We demonstrate the methods by considering two systems. For a single-component system, we perform a comparison between the results derived from the collineations and those obtained using the coarse-grained bond-orientational order parameters. Further, we illustrate the methods by considering the crystallization process in a Wahnstr\"{o}m binary $BA_2$ system forming a rather complex crystal structure.

### Paper [53]
Title: Renormalization-group analysis of two-dimensional long-range XY models with $p$-fold anisotropy: the $\sigma=2$ boundary
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29362
Authors: Xianzhi Pan, Chengxiang Ding, Youjin Deng
Abstract: The two-dimensional classical XY model with $1/r^{2+\sigma}$ interactions has recently been shown to undergo, at the marginal case $\sigma=2$, a continuous transition into a logarithmic quasi-long-range-ordered (log-QLRO) phase where the correlation decays as $C(r) \sim (\ln r)^{-\eta_\ell}$. We study how a $p$-fold field $h_p\cos(p\theta)$ modifies this picture. Using the physical vortex and $p$-fold field fugacities, we construct a unified set of renormalization-group (RG) equations that interpolate between the short-range (SR) and long-range (LR) cases through an LR coupling parameter $\kappa$. For $p<4$, the small-$h_p$ perturbative treatment breaks down and the critical behavior lies beyond the present RG treatment. For $p>4$, the field is irrelevant at the pure fixed point, but at low temperature the LR enhancement drives the stiffness upward: log-QLRO appears only as a finite-scale crossover, and the only transition is the continuous one from the disordered into the $p$-fold-ordered phase. For $p=4$, the fixed line shifts from the SR Ashkin-Teller form, and the transition into the fourfold-ordered phase has a nonuniversal correlation-length exponent depending on both $\kappa$ and $h_4$. These results support the $\sigma_*=2$ boundary, in contrast to Sak's criterion.

### Paper [54]
Title: Auditing a superconducting database built by a large language model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29445
Authors: Wenjian Lu
Abstract: The data on superconducting materials are published as measurements and scattered across the literature, in prose, in tables and in figures, so that gathering them into one place means reading the papers themselves. Large language models (LLMs) can read those papers, and databases built that way are appearing.
