The difficulty is not the reading but the trust: a number lifted out of a sentence can belong to a neighbouring compound, a computed transition temperature can be recorded without saying that it is computed, and the paper itself may be wrong. This paper describes a pipeline that extracts superconducting materials from the published literature with an LLM, checks every value against its source, and asks how far that checking goes. The pipeline proposed 19,576 records; a cascade of gates rejected 659,252 field-level extractions, 644,438 of them for want of verbatim evidence, 13,121 because the text does not attach the value to the material, and 1,693 because the source sentence reads as a calculation. The database that survives holds 3,137 material records, each with the sentence its value came from. An audit of the survivors by two independent models, on 500 records drawn in proportion to the database, shows that the gates remove the errors that are mechanical: in 93.4% of the audited records the value is in the text and the text is about the compound the record names, and 91.8% of them are measurements rather than predictions.

### Paper [55]
Title: Fully solid-state all-2D micro-supercapacitors for on-chip energy storage
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29476
Authors: Bar Favelukis, Andreas Furchner, Barak Ratzker, Yiftach Kushnir, Asaf Nitsan, Rebeca Miyar, Yugal Rathod, Alexander Upcher, Nitzan Maman, Pini Shekhter, Varun Natu, Maxim Sokol
Abstract: Integrating energy storage directly into microelectronic platforms remains a major challenge. Although solid-state micro-supercapacitors (MSCs) have been previously demonstrated, most still rely on liquid or solvent-containing gel electrolytes, which require additional encapsulation and complicate device integration. Here, we demonstrate wafer-scale fabrication of all-2D, fully solid-state MSCs by combining conductive Ti3C2Tz MXene electrodes with a natural vermiculite (VMT) clay solid electrolyte. Both materials are solution-processable and can be independently patterned using microelectronics compatible fabrication techniques, allowing for their integration across 4-inch Si/SiO2 wafers. The resulting devices showcase an active stack of only 28 nm thick, with an areal capacitance of 205 uF/cm2 and volumetric energy density of ~15.5 mWh/cm3. Notably, this work introduces a patterned 2D clay as a solid electrolyte in an on-chip electrochemical energy-storage device, extending the role of layered materials beyond electrodes to ion-conducting components. To investigate device behavior and degradation, we employ non-destructive imaging ellipsometry to map individual layer thicknesses and extract the spatial distribution of MXene conductivity across the chip. This approach reveals localized changes in electrode properties following prolonged electrochemical cycling, providing insight into degradation processes that are difficult to capture using conventional spatially averaged measurements. Together, these results establish a route towards fully solid-state, all-2D energy-storage devices and highlight the potential of solution-processable layered materials for integration into microelectronic systems.

### Paper [56]
Title: Population of spin orbit-split-electron branches in semimetallic HgTe quantum wells
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29487
Authors: G. M. Minkov, O. E. Rut, A. A. Sherstobitov, A. V. Germanenko, S. A. Dvoretski, N. N. Mikhailov
Abstract: We present the results of experimental studies on magnetoresistance, the Hall effect, and Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations performed in gated heterostructures with HgTe-based gapless semiconductor quantum wells. We show that in quantum wells with widths corresponding to a semimetallic charge carrier spectrum, electrons, in contrast to the idealized model, populate both spin-orbit-split branches of the spectrum. This occurs regardless of whether the Fermi level lies exclusively in the conduction band or within the conduction-valence band overlap region.

### Paper [57]
Title: Ground-State Manifold Curvature and Phase Transitions in the Kitaev Honeycomb Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29495
Authors: Omid Yahyayi Monem, Setare Bashari, Hosein Mohammadzadeh
Abstract: The Kitaev honeycomb model is an exactly solvable spin model whose ground state realizes a quantum spin liquid with gapped Abelian phases and a gapless phase that becomes a non-Abelian phase in a magnetic field. Here we study its phase transitions through the geometry of the ground-state manifold, using the Ricci scalar of the quantum metric. In the three gapped phases we obtain closed-form expressions for the curvature, which is negative and diverges at the critical lines; the three phases are related to one another by an exact change of coordinates. In the gapless phase, the curvature of the infrared-regularized metric is positive and decays inversely with the logarithm of the system size, with a size-independent amplitude that diverges at the phase boundaries, with an exponent different from that of the gapped side. The change of sign and the divergences together reproduce the complete phase diagram. A magnetic field gaps out the gapless phase and plays the role of the infrared cutoff, so that away from the critical lines the curvature of the non-Abelian phase becomes finite and positive, while around the critical lines it opens a crossover region whose width scales quadratically with the field. Since divergences of geometric quantities are often regarded as signatures of phase transitions, the divergence of the Ricci scalar at the critical lines provides a geometric signature of these transitions.

### Paper [58]
Title: FlakeForge: automated lithography-ready device layout from optical images of exfoliated van der Waals flakes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29509
Authors: Or Berman, Ilay Mangel
Abstract: Fabricating a device from an exfoliated van der Waals flake requires finding a suitable flake under a microscope and designing its lithography layout by hand. We present FlakeForge, an open-source pipeline from raw optical images of a marker-patterned chip to a lithography-ready, multilayer design file. It scores flakes for device usability, aligns and scales each image from its markers, fits the user's device design to each selected flake's contour and connects every contact to a bond pad by a trace routed around all earlier devices, so no two overlap. Demonstrated on Hall bars and Little-Parks rings, it processed four chips in about half an hour, work that took an expert days. It runs fully unattended or pauses for user review. Adapting it to a new material, marker design or device dimensions requires only configuration, with no training data; a new device type requires only its geometry definition.

### Paper [59]
Title: Engineering strategies for high-performance self-powered zinc oxide (ZnO) ultraviolet photodetectors: a design-oriented review
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29515
Authors: Eka Nurfani, Keysya C. A. Putri, Tarmizi Taher, Aditya Rianjanu, Muhamad F. Arif
Abstract: Self-powered ultraviolet (UV) photodetectors generate a signal at zero applied bias, removing standby power and suppressing dark current, suiting battery-free Internet-of-Things sensing. Zinc oxide (ZnO) is the archetypal absorber for this task: UV-selective, earth-abundant, non-toxic, and processable at low temperature from solution. Operating it without bias, however, requires a built-in asymmetry that pristine ZnO rarely provides. Because the literature is organized by material or mechanism, the design logic remains obscured. Here we organize the field by engineering strategy, identifying five families of levers (junction and built-in-field engineering, doping and defect control, polarization coupling, interface/surface and photoelectrochemical engineering, and architecture and light management) and mapping each onto the metrics it serves and the trade-offs it imposes. A unifying lens is the zero-bias doping paradox: raising carrier density narrows the depletion region that zero-bias collection needs, so the self-powered optimum differs from the biased one. We flag the detectivity, speed, and active-area pitfalls that make zero-bias performance easy to overstate, and condense the synthesis into a decision tree linking target metric to strategy and growth route. The remaining bottlenecks (persistent photoconductivity, p-type ZnO, and reporting consistency) are problems of defect control and rigorous measurement, not of new materials.

### Paper [60]
Title: Exact superdiffusion exponents and hull exploration of chiral random walkers on critical percolation clusters
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29537
Authors: Joshua Uhlig, Ralf Metzler, Erik Kalz
Abstract: Generalizing de Gennes' ant-in-the-labyrinth concept, we consider a chiral random walker on a percolation cluster at criticality. The fully chiral ant clings to obstacle boundaries and traces the fractal hulls of the cluster, spreading with the exact walk dimension $7/4$ until its orbit closes. Because the cluster contains holes on all scales, the ensemble spreads superdiffusively, $\langle r^2\rangle\sim t^{89/84}$, carried by a diminishing fraction of walkers whose orbits are still open. Both exponents follow from percolation geometry alone, and agree well with large-scale numerical simulations. When the walker turns chirally only with probability $\chi<1$, superdiffusion gives way to the subdiffusion of the ant at a crossover time set by both exponents, with a diffusion coefficient enhanced by $(1-\chi)^{-2/d_w}$. The relation between crossover time and diffusion coefficient does not depend on how chirality is realized, offering a direct test for chiral tracers in disordered media.

### Paper [61]
Title: AmorphGen: A Python package for amorphous structure generation with machine-learning and classical interatomic potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29539
Authors: Chaiyawat Kaewmeechai, Louie Slocombe, Alexis G. Manche, David O. Scanlon
Abstract: AmorphGen is an open-source Python package for generating and analyzing amorphous structures from a composition or a crystal. Chemically informed rules estimate initial densities and assign separation and coordination constraints. Three routes are available: random placement followed by relaxation, melt-quench from a crystal, and a hybrid route that anneals and quenches random structures. ASE provides access to classical and machine-learning potentials, with GPU batching for selected backends. Analysis tools compute coordination numbers, bond angles, rings, and X-ray or neutron structure factors for comparison with measurements. An optional interface to the FEFF code computes extended X-ray absorption fine structure (EXAFS) spectra. For amorphous Ga2O3, TiO2 and InGaZnO4, the cation coordination numbers are close to reference models and measurements, and the X-ray structure factors reproduce the main measured peaks. The EXAFS spectra for Ga2O3 and InGaZnO4 also capture the dominant first-shell features observed experimentally. The hybrid route reduces bonding defects in SiO2 and As and builds the first sharp diffraction peak of SiO2. By simplifying ensemble generation and standardizing structural assessment, AmorphGen makes it practical to compare amorphous models across compositions, potentials, and preparation routes, and to screen new amorphous compositions.

### Paper [62]
Title: Ballistic propagation-mediated super-resolution light-element imaging: in-situ tracking dynamic hydrogen migration
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29563
Authors: Zhiyao Liang, Feng Liu, Linyuan Chen, Zhiwei Lv, Shiya Chen, Xinyu Liao, Yuzhou He, Yi Wang, Pengpeng Liu, Hua Bing Tao, Nianpeng Lu, Changlin Zheng, Yang Sun, Qinghua Zhang, Rafal E. Dunin-Borkowski, Xian-Kui Wei
Abstract: Transmission electrons - with few-picometer wavelengths - can theoretically resolve all atoms in solids. However, constrained by factors such as lens aberrations, Z-contrast dependence and poor temporal resolution, the scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) fails to tackle the long-standing challenge of hydrogen dynamics on atomic scale. Here, our experiment-guided simulations reveal that unlike heavy-atom imaging - governed by wave-based imaging process - ballistic propagation of particle-like electrons in Cs-corrected TEM enables super-resolution imaging (SRI) of light atoms, achieving a resolution down to 14 pm and a robust hydrogen contrast of ~24%. Our in-situ SRI study not only uncovers polymetric hydrogen aggregation in TiHx and H-LaAlO3, but also inter-site migration, rotational displacement of hydrogen species and collective hydrogen tunneling-induced polarization surge in the oxide. By exploiting the novel electron-matter interactions, our findings offer an unparalleled approach to probe hydrogen dynamics and establish accurate structure-property relationships in diverse functional materials.

### Paper [63]
Title: MagPot: A magnetoelastic potential generator for molecular dynamics simulations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29574
Authors: P. Nieves, I. Korniienko, S. Arapan, A. Fraile, R. Iglesias, D. Legut
Abstract: Magnetoelastic phenomena originate from the coupling between atomic displacements and magnetic degrees of freedom, and their atomistic simulation requires interatomic models in which magnetic interactions depend explicitly on interatomic distance. This paper presents MagPot, a Python program that generates Bethe-Slater parameter sets for spin-lattice molecular dynamics simulations using the SPIN package of the Large-scale Atomic/Molecular Massively Parallel Simulator (LAMMPS). From a compact set of material properties, the program determines the radial functions describing Heisenberg exchange and the dipole and quadrupole contributions of the two-ion N\'eel interaction. Version 1.0 supports simple-cubic, body-centred-cubic and face-centred-cubic lattices with ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic configurations. A central feature of MagPot is its modular architecture, which allows the magnetic contribution it generates to be combined additively with a broad range of compatible non-magnetic interatomic potentials. The parameterisation workflow is assessed through equation-of-state, Curie-temperature, volume-magnetostriction, pressure-dependent magnetocrystalline-anisotropy and anisotropic-magnetoelastic tests. By automating the construction of the magnetic contribution to the interatomic model, MagPot offers a straightforward and reproducible route from material properties to LAMMPS-ready magnetic interactions, facilitating large-scale atomistic simulations of magnetoelasticity and magnetostriction, as well as physical phenomena associated with magnon-phonon coupling.

### Paper [64]
Title: Kinetic inductance of two-dimensional viscous electron fluid
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29645
Authors: A. A. Grigorev, A. N. Afanasiev
Abstract: Modern high-mobility two-dimensional electron systems are pure enough to manifest hydrodynamic regime of electron transport similar to dynamics of classical fluids. With the bulk scattering controlled by momentum-conserving electron-electron collisions, these novel hydrodynamic materials demonstrate systematic violation of conventional metallic behavior due to the Drude model in stationary electric transport. In this letter, we study the linear reactive AC response of 2D electron fluid in a narrow channel geometry and show that the essentially non-local viscous dissipation induces non-trivial size effect in it. In particular, kinetic inductance, which represents collective inertia of the entire electron fluid, is enhanced by non-uniform flow profile and, in contrast to diffusive transport, dominates the electrical impedance already in collisional regime by virtue of viscous skin effect. The onset of reactive behavior falls within the microwave frequency domain in the state-of-the-art 2D hydrodynamic conductors which makes them promising material system for applications in high-frequency electronics at elevated temperatures.

### Paper [65]
Title: Geometry Reorganizes Nonequilibrium States that Gate Topological Switching in an Active Polymer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29649
Authors: Shiqi Zhou
Abstract: Even under identical external conditions, a driven system can occupy distinct long-lived states with different susceptibilities to the same rare transition. We demonstrate this in a confined partially active polymer, where independent unknotted replicas occupy distinct mechanical states with different topological futures. Matched-control and event-resolved analyses reveal a susceptible reservoir and a short threading precursor. We test this state dependence prospectively by launching 2272 Langevin-noise branches from 71 preserved unknotted phase-space states using a classifier prespecified from independent data. Most strikingly, the same classifier strongly separates finite-time knotting at one pore radius, yet reverses its susceptibility ordering at a neighboring radius. Thus geometry does not merely shift an average knotting rate; it selects among long-lived nonequilibrium states and reorganizes the mapping between a pre-existing mechanical coordinate and future topological fate.

### Paper [66]
Title: Elastic crumple pair potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29654
Authors: D. Gimeno, E. Hamm, J. A. Hanna
Abstract: Thin structures across scales and materials, from graphene sheets to clothing to aircraft skins, respond to forcing and confinement by crumpling, with deformation localized into zones of characteristic shape. Much attention has been paid to isolated crescent vertex and ridge shapes, but there has been almost no investigation into the dynamics, creation/destruction, and interactions between multiple vertices (``crumples'') that are inherent in nearly all deformation processes. The next step beyond single crumples are pair interactions, mediated by background curvature. Of these, we focus on two common types, using a quasi-1D setup allowing controlled boundary motion and force measurement coupled with tracking of crumple positions. We provide experimental evidence for non-monotonic pair potentials, with short-range attraction, long-range repulsion, and an intermediate forbidden region. We construct a phenomenological energy that reproduces the bifurcations and the interrelations between observed quantities. These results provide a new insight and input towards understanding and building reduced descriptions of dynamic deformation and pattern formation and evolution in thin elastic plate and shell structures.

### Paper [67]
Title: Giant magnetic anisotropy in layered polar metal Sr$_3$Co$_2$O$_7$ from Charge-disproportionation orbital unquenching
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29742
Authors: Houssam Sabri, Hong-Fei Huang, Jie-Xiang Yu, Jiadong Zang
Abstract: Crystal-field quenching usually suppresses orbital angular momentum and limits magnetic anisotropy in transition-metal oxides. Here we identify charge disproportionation as a route to giant magnetocrystalline anisotropy in the layered polar metal Sr$_3$Co$_2$O$_7$. First-principles calculations reveal an energy preference of 234.0 meV per cell for in-plane [110] magnetization over [001]. Charge disproportionation and the associated polar displacement reconstruct the local cobalt crystal fields, creating inequivalent sites with distinct orbital hierarchies. This reconstruction enables a strongly directional orbital response, particularly at the displaced cobalt site, where orbital angular momentum is recovered for in-plane magnetization. Spin--orbit coupling converts this response into a large magnetic anisotropic energy. In the centrosymmetric reference, the orbital moments remain nearly quenched and the anisotropy is strongly reduced. A local Green-function and orbital-moment analysis connects the large energy scale to orbital unquenching, while the conventional second-order contribution is small within the adopted approximation. These results identify charge-disproportionation-driven orbital reconstruction as a design principle for large anisotropy and motivate layered perovskites and magnetic polar metals as platforms in the search for rare-earth-free permanent magnets.

### Paper [68]
Title: Exact many-body scars of generic spin models from singlet covers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29754
Authors: Camilla Polvara, William Witczak-Krempa
Abstract: The square-lattice transverse-field Ising model has exact zero-energy eigenstates at any field: products of singlets on alternate plaquette diagonals. A longitudinal field isolates them from a symmetry-protected degeneracy. The mechanism is that a site coupled equally to both ends of a singlet annihilates it. The result thus holds for any spin $s$, anisotropic exchange, a large class of bond disorder and certain three-spin terms. This mechanism decides which lattices host such states: two of the 11 Archimedean lattices (four at $s=1/2$), bcc and quasicrystals with spins on the edges. Singlet pumping cools any state into them.

### Paper [69]
Title: Principles of Material Memory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29774
Authors: Vladimir A. Baulin
Abstract: Materials keep traces of how they were driven: a field leaves a magnetization behind, and a sequence of forces can leave a pattern that records the order in which they acted. It remains open what such a history records, which interactions create the record and which dynamics preserve it. This review relates the three. Forces applied in different orders can leave different states after the drive is removed, and in a spatial network the preferred local arrangements may not be reconcilable around a closed loop. Both effects compare two paths, one in time and one around a loop in space, and both are described by commutators, curvature and holonomy. Restoring forces and fluctuations then set how long the trace survives: a displacement relaxes, a phase diffuses, and barriers protect a pattern against rare thermal transitions. Near an instability, a weak writing bias is amplified while the coordinate that carries the state has no restoring force, and the barriers that retain the chosen state form only afterwards. The choice is therefore as accurate as the detection of the bias against the noise during the time for which the coordinate is free. Once a state is stored behind a barrier, a static bias replaces it through the same thermal channel by which it would be lost. The ratio of the mean retention time to the mean rewriting time is then bounded, up to a prefactor close to one, by the Boltzmann factor of an effective energy bias, whatever the height of the barrier. Calculations for capillary rotors and point-dipole arrays show how the geometry of the interactions constrains the patterns that a material can store. In a model of three coupled amplitudes, a weak bias makes two mirror-image patterns lose stability at slightly different coupling strengths, so that a coupling pulse between the two thresholds erases one pattern and preserves the other.

### Paper [70]
Title: A phenomenological decomposition of SRC-inspired occupation effects in imbalanced Fermi superfluids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29791
Authors: Lucas A. Souza
Abstract: Imbalanced two-component Fermi superfluids are usually formulated with sharp Fermi-step occupations, although interacting systems exhibit depletion below the Fermi surface and a high-momentum tail. We introduce a sector-resolved phenomenological framework in which one prescribed short-range-correlation (SRC)-inspired momentum distribution modifies either the anomalous pairing contribution (model P), the imbalance-induced blocking term (model B), or both (model P+B). The four realizations---ideal, P, B, and P+B---are compared through the grand-potential landscape and through a fixed-density reconstruction of the paired/phase-separated to polarized-normal boundary in three and two dimensions. In both dimensionalities, the pairing-sector modification accounts for nearly all of the reduction in the critical polarization, whereas the blocking-sector correction remains subdominant. Consequently, the phase boundaries form two nearly degenerate pairs, ideal $\simeq$ B and P $\simeq$ P+B. These results quantify, within the stated phenomenological construction, how a prescribed SRC-inspired occupation propagates separately through the pairing and blocking sectors of an imbalanced superfluid.

### Paper [71]
Title: Inferring Experimental Objectives in Microscopy Experiments from Decision Traces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29814
Authors: Kamyar Barakati, Boris N. Slautin, Aditya Raghavan, Babita Babita, Surya Prakash Reddy, Jordan Marshall, Munireach Nannory, Stephen Puplampu, Alla Slautina, Faith M. Hines, Newsha Javanmardi, Sergei V. Kalinin
Abstract: Modern microscopes can acquire, analyze and act on data far faster than human operators can decide what to measure next. Exploiting this speed in automated experiments requires the instrument to follow the scientific logic of the operator, and one way to encode that logic is through reward functions that become part of the operation process. Here we explore whether such reward functions can be learned from the decision histories of microscope operators. We propose multiple frameworks in which recorded choices among alternative measurement locations serve as comparative evidence about the operator's preferences, and we compare several algorithms for reconstructing the reward including interpretable linear models built on physically meaningful image descriptors, nonlinear functions of the same descriptors, an unsupervised image representation followed by preference modeling, and a preference trained deep kernel inspired model. The result of this analysis is a spatial field of reward functions over the sample that can be used to run the instrument. We also propose a way to benchmark these approaches, based on withheld decisions and on decision histories generated with known objectives. We then examine the possibility that a decision history contains changing priorities throughout the experiment. A hidden Markov model assigns distinct rewards to recurring selection strategies, whereas a continuous-state model tracks smoothly varying preference weights. Controlled sequences generated over real microscopy candidate sets show that both concealed switches and gradual drift can be recovered. These reward functions, learned directly from recorded decisions, provide candidate objectives for future automated microscopy experiments.

### Paper [72]
Title: Mixed-State Magnetotransport Below $T_c$ in Type-II Superconducting NbN Thin Films
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29817
Authors: Sai Vaishnavi Kanduri, Danilo B\"urger, Zichao Li, Mario Ziegler, Detlef Born, Sahitya V. Vegesna, Heidemarie Kr\"uger
Abstract: The transport properties of type-II superconductors in the mixed state below the superconducting transition temperature, $T_c$, remain difficult to interpret because superconducting and dissipative processes coexist in the presence of a magnetic field. To investigate this regime in NbN thin films, we measured the field-dependent sheet resistance ($R^M_{xx}$) and Hall resistance ($R^M_{xy}$) below $T_c$ in Van der Pauw geometry for 4-nm-thick NbN films on sapphire and silicon substrates at driving currents of 1 mA and 2 mA. By combining the longitudinal response with the antisymmetrized Hall signal, we use a phenomenological current-partition model to estimate the dissipative and superconducting current contributions. From this analysis, we estimate the field-dependent superconducting current together with the Hall-derived effective dissipative carrier density and mobility. We also observe an even-in-field transverse signal whose magnitude depends strongly on the driving current. We discuss this signal as a possible mixed-state response involving the magnetic and supercurrent texture of Abrikosov vortices, vortex dynamics, and Cooper-pair current redistribution. The present measurements do not uniquely distinguish vortex, condensate, or combined contributions. The model provides a practical way to compare the longitudinal and transverse mixed-state responses without implying a direct spatial separation of the two current channels.

### Paper [73]
Title: Sondheimer oscillations in Weyl semimetal films
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29875
Authors: Lucas M\'echet, Louis-Thomas Gendron, Ion Garate
Abstract: In conducting films subject to an out-of-plane magnetic field, electron motion between the surfaces gives rise to conductance oscillations periodic in magnetic field, known as Sondheimer oscillations. While this size effect has been investigated in conventional metals, its dependence on the Berry curvature of topological materials remains less explored. In this work, we extend the semiclassical Boltzmann description of Sondheimer oscillations to type-I Weyl semimetal films. We account for the Berry curvature, the orbital magnetic moment and the effect of the Zeeman coupling on the Fermi wavevector and the electronic group velocity. The Berry curvature and the orbital magnetic moment generate a beating pattern and chirping in the Sondheimer oscillations. In the thick-film and thin-film regimes, we provide analytical expressions for the longitudinal conductivity, which highlight the interplay between electronic band topology and boundary-scattering in magnetotransport.

### Paper [74]
Title: Unveiling the Complex high-pressure phases of Sodium at the Melting Curve Minimum via Data-Drive Crystal Structure Prediction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29889
Authors: Zhenyu Wang, Chengqian Zhang, Xiaoyang Wang, Haiyang Niu, Jian Lv, Han Wang
Abstract: Sodium exhibits a remarkable concentration of complex crystalline phases near its melting-curve minimum, yet the atomic arrangements of several experimentally observed phases remain unresolved. Here, we combine CALYPSO crystal structure prediction with Deep Potential models developed through concurrent learning to explore this structural landscape. Searches over more than 1.6 million configurations recover the previously reported $I4/m$ structure of $tI50$ and identify candidate atomic arrangements for $oC120$ and $aP90$, together with three additional low-enthalpy structures, $tI52$, $oP72$, and $oP92$. The proposed candidates reproduce key features of the experimentally indexed cell geometries. First-principles enthalpy calculations, Gibbs free-energy comparisons, and solid--liquid coexistence simulations reveal close competition among these structures and established sodium phases. Near the calculated reference melting minimum, $tI50$ is favored over $cI16$ and $oP8$, whereas $oC120$ and $aP90$ remain closely competitive, with their relative stability sensitive to model accuracy. These results provide testable structural models for unresolved experimental phases and reveal multiple closely competing low-enthalpy configurations near sodium's melting minimum

### Paper [75]
Title: Pyroelectric Harvesting from Steady Heat Sources via Active Piezoelectric Thermal Switching
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29928
Authors: Jun Usami, Yuki Okamoto, Shimpei Saito, Saeko Tachikawa, Soumei Baba, Takeshi Fujii, Sucheta Gorwadkar, Natsumi Makimoto, Takeshi Kobayashi
Abstract: This study demonstrates pyroelectric energy harvesting from a steady heat source using active piezoelectric thermal switching in a PZT/Si bimorph cantilever. The same PZT layer serves as both the mechanical actuator and the pyroelectric energy-conversion element. Dual output electrodes enable separation of the pyroelectric and piezoelectric signals. Pyroelectric energy harvesting is experimentally demonstrated at frequencies up to 110 Hz and temperature differences as small as 5 K. Comparison with the response predicted from the independently measured pyroelectric coefficient, together with transient thermal simulations, identifies dynamic interfacial heat transfer as a key limitation to high-frequency operation. This work demonstrates a compact MEMS approach for higher-frequency pyroelectric energy harvesting from low-grade steady heat sources.

### Paper [76]
Title: Nonmonotonic In-Plane Thermal Transport in Metal-Embedded Graphitic Carbon Nitride
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30015
Authors: Wenwu Jiang, Hekai Bu, Wei Qiu, Yong Ni
Abstract: Embedding metal atoms within the intrinsic cavities of graphitic carbon nitride (g-C3N4) modifies its local bonding and vibrational properties, yet its consequences for lattice thermal transport remain poorly understood. Here, we combine a machine-learned potential (MLP) with homogeneous nonequilibrium molecular dynamics (HNEMD) to investigate in-plane thermal transport in Cu-embedded g-C3N4 across the full range of cavity occupation. The thermal conductivity exhibits a pronounced nonmonotonic dependence on Cu occupation, decreasing at intermediate occupation and partially recovering as the cavities approach full occupation. Frequency-resolved analysis reveals that this behavior originates from the competition between two phonon contributions. The low-frequency vibrations introduced by Cu couple to the intrinsic modes of g-C3N4, reconstructing the phonon dispersion and suppressing thermal transport below 10 THz. With increasing Cu occupation, the collective mechanical response of the N atoms surrounding the cavities is enhanced, accompanied by increased phonon group velocities in the 20-30 THz range and a corresponding recovery of the high-frequency thermal contribution. The competition between the saturating low-frequency suppression and the increasing high-frequency contribution gives rise to the nonmonotonic thermal conductivity. A similar occupation dependence and spectral redistribution are observed for Ag-embedded g-C3N4. These results identify competing phonon contributions as the microscopic origin of nonmonotonic thermal transport in metal-embedded g-C3N4 and suggest that occupation of intrinsic cavities provides a route to redistributing phonon transport in porous crystalline materials.

### Paper [77]
Title: Geometric Polarization-Virial Theorems for Field-Induced Dipolar Interactions and Force Reversal
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30039
Authors: Shuze Zhu
Abstract: Electric fields can turn attraction into repulsion between far-separated atoms, yet the complete angular mechanical structure governing this reversal has remained unknown. Here, we uncover an entropy-like geometric structure: the cubically weighted interaction free energy, r^3 F(r), is nondecreasing under physical dilation. This structure unifies two exact polarization-virial theorems for arbitrary stable quasistatic dipole assemblies and fully retarded dipole pairs under stated conditions. Consequently, the free energy has at most one zero and energetic cancellation precedes mechanical cancellation. A force zero retains strictly positive energy, whereas an energy zero retains an attractive force. The resulting analytical angular fine structure is resolved by a quantum harmonic oscillator pair, yielding the complete arbitrary-angle interaction energy and radial force that recover quantum-perturbative results. The conventional dipolar magic angle splits into strictly ordered energy- and force-zero angles; the latter uniquely bounds the radially repulsive sector and carries a positive energy barrier. Derived scaling laws and finite-sphere calculations provide experimentally testable signatures from microscopic dipoles to macroscopic polarizable bodies.

### Paper [78]
Title: Domain advantage in two-dimensional quantum-device design with multi-modal agentic harness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30043
Authors: Shenhao Miao, Yizhou Li, Liqiao Xia, Ruihao Liu, Dingke Wang, Pablo Jarillo-Herrero, Eli Zeldov, Jiaqi Cai
Abstract: High-quality two-dimensional quantum devices are commonly fabricated from mechanically exfoliated crystals, whose irregular shapes and placement require a lithographic layout tailored to each specimen. Drawing these layouts remains largely manual. We introduce qlaybench, a benchmark that tests whether language-model agents can turn microscope images and experimental requirements into device layouts that fit the specimen. Agents with conventional tools often construct devices when material outlines are supplied, but struggle to identify those outlines from images. Modelled on the human workflow, qlaybot, a multimodal agentic harness, connects image interpretation to the tools used to align images, draw device features and inspect the resulting layout. We evaluate each layout with checks drawn from practical design experience, asking whether it fits the specimen and connects its intended components. With greater computational effort, an agent using qlaybot passes 81.5% of the benchmark tasks, compared with 24.1% when it uses conventional tools. This domain advantage highlights the importance of connecting scientific interpretation of a specimen to geometric design and provides a route towards reducing manual effort in two-dimensional quantum-device fabrication.

### Paper [79]
Title: Trajectory arclength reveals thermodynamic criticality
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30053
Authors: Jim\'enez-L\'opez,  Javier,  Garc\'ia-Garrido, V. J
Abstract: Thermodynamic criticality arises at continuous phase transitions, where collective interactions produce non-analytic behavior in equilibrium thermodynamic quantities in the thermodynamic limit. We show that this criticality also leaves an analytically predictable signature in the arclength of microscopic Hamiltonian trajectories. For the attractive Hamiltonian mean-field model, we derive the thermodynamic limit of the equilibrium-averaged arclength, formulated as a normalized Lagrangian descriptor, and demonstrate that it develops a kink precisely at the phase transition. We trace this non-analyticity to spontaneous magnetization and derive the leading critical finite-size correction, scaling as $N^{-1/2}$ with an explicit analytical prefactor. Simulations with up to $10^5$ degrees of freedom confirm both predictions without adjustable parameters. Requiring neither tangent-space evolution nor prior specification of an order parameter for its computation, trajectory arclength provides a simple, quantitative link between microscopic Hamiltonian motion and collective thermodynamic criticality.

### Paper [80]
Title: Solids activated by multi-body nonreciprocity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30058
Authors: Brady Wu, Daniel S. Seara, Qinghao Mao, Vincenzo Vitelli, Heinrich M. Jaeger
Abstract: Field-mediated forces between particles can be both nonreciprocal and irreducibly multi-body, giving rise to emergent activity in systems of individually passive particles. While prior work extensively studied how multi-body nonreciprocity can activate few-body systems, fuel intermittency, and drive liquid-like behavior, the effect on the solid state of large particle ensembles is less explored. Here we use two-dimensional, close-packed rafts of identical, athermal, acoustically levitated microspheres to show how multi-body nonreciprocity injects activity in solids. At the level of both individual particles and collective dynamics, crystalline rafts exhibit persistent circulations with no net handedness. The rafts also exhibit a phonon-like dispersion relation corresponding to a large Poisson ratio, indicating an induced softness to shear. Within the bulk, lattice symmetry masks microscopic nonreciprocity, although it can manifest in renormalized, reciprocal elastic coefficients. At dislocations and raft edges, where this symmetry is broken, multi-body nonreciprocal interactions act as local generators of activity. A coarse-grained microscopic model of multi-body nonreciprocity, calibrated on three-particle simulations, recovers the rafts' experimentally observed dispersion relations and localized activity. Using these findings, we illustrate how to harness defect-induced activity for programmable movement by arranging microscopic dislocations to drive macroscopic propulsion and rotation of a whole raft.

### Paper [81]
Title: NEP-Spin: A spin-dependent neuroevolution potential for large-scale spin--lattice simulations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30076
Authors: Chengbing Chen, Zheyong Fan, Ben Xu
Abstract: NEP-Spin is a \glsentrylong{nep} for coupled atomic motion and variable-length magnetic moments. Local bond and spin tensors give an energy invariant under independent spin and spatial rotations without \glsentrylong{soc} and under joint rotations with it. We benchmark energies, atomic forces, and magnetic forces on public datasets for collinear Fe--Al and noncollinear Fe. In multiferroic \glsentrylong{bfo}, the predicted zero-field cycloid period is 69.7~nm, compared with 62--63~nm from neutron scattering. An electric field of 20~mV/\AA{} opposite to the polarization lengthens the period to 93.2~nm, while imposing the same polar displacement at zero field gives 85.1~nm. In a uniform antiferromagnetic cell at fixed lattice vectors, 6~T magnetic fields along orthogonal basal axes reorient the Fe moments and give opposite signs of the ionic polarization change $\Delta P_y$. First-principles ionic relaxations at the predicted moment vectors reproduce both signs. In a 2.79-million-atom ferroelectric twin, the cycloid rotation planes tilt by different amounts from their shared initial plane and remain distinct over 10~ps at 100~K. At 10~ps the propagation axes lie within about $7^\circ$ of their initial directions. With inertial spin dynamics, GPUMD advances a 5.93-million-atom BiFeO$_3$ cell at 21.8 million atom-steps per second on four RTX 4090 GPUs.

### Paper [82]
Title: Valence band, electron correlation, and possible temperature dependent change in the electronic structure near $E_F$ of a candidate of ambient-pressure nickelate superconductors Sr$_3$Ni$_2$O$_5$Cl$_2$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30088
Authors: Ryota Goban, Daiki Ootsuki, Kenichi Ozawa, Akira Yasui, Masayuki Ochi, Kazuhiko Kuroki, Ryo Matsumoto, Kensei Terashima, Yoshihiko Takano, Yuji Muraoka, Takayoshi Yokoya
Abstract: Photoemission spectroscopy (PES) studies of Sr$_3$Ni$_2$O$_5$Cl$_2$ (Sr3252), a candidate ambient-pressure bilayer nickelate superconductor, have been performed to investigate the electronic structure. Valence band spectra, which are compared with DFT and DFT+U calculations, reveal dominant Ni 3$d$ $t_{2g}$-derived states located around 0.6 eV binding energy with a shoulder around 0.2 eV that can be attributed to states with a dominant Ni 3$d_{3z2-r2}$ character. The overall valence band shape is better reproduced by the simulated spectrum of DFT+U (= 3 eV), providing an experimental estimation of the strength of electron correlation. High-resolution PES near the Fermi level ($E_F$) exhibits marked temperature dependence, which cannot be fully understood by a broadening due to coupling of a photo-hole to phonons alone and suggests change in electronic structure. The results provide fundamental electronic structure information of Sr3252, which is useful to further explore the superconductivity of Sr3252.

### Paper [83]
Title: Soliton Propagation of Zero Sound in Superfluid $^3$He-B
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30108
Authors: J. A. Sauls
Abstract: Nonlinear equations for zero sound interacting with the B-phase collective modes are derived by treating $^3$He-B as a medium of two-level ``molecules'' (Cooper pairs), The nonlinear equations reduce to known results from kinetic theory in the linear response limit. Soliton solutions to the nonlinear equations are discussed in context with the experiments of Polturak et al.

### Paper [84]
Title: Indentation-induced polar domain reconfiguration in the charge-density-wave semiconductor EuTe4
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30121
Authors: Hidefumi Takahashi, Yuki Umewaka, Kiichiro Yoshida, Ritsuto Nakamura, Akitoshi Nakano, Takao Matsumura, Daiki Sekine, Masakazu Matsubara, Masayuki Ochi, Shintaro Ishiwata
Abstract: Controlling polar domains in conducting materials by external fields could unlock polarization-based electronic functionalities, yet remains experimentally challenging. While mechanical actuation driven by local strain, stress, or strain gradients has been proposed as an external-voltage-free route, its experimental demonstration in relatively conducting polar materials remains limited. Here we demonstrate mechanically induced polar-domain reconfiguration in the charge-density-wave (CDW) semiconductor EuTe4 at room temperature, whose resistivity is on the order of 10 m$\Omega$cm. Polarized-light microscopy and second-harmonic-generation measurements reveal CDW-induced in-plane polarization domains with an approximately 90 $^\circ$ domain configuration. Local indentation with a $\sim$1 $\mu$m-apex-radius needle reconfigures these domains, producing write/erase-like changes in wedge-shaped regions. These results establish local mechanical-field domain engineering in a relatively conducting CDW semiconductor, with possible contributions from both ferroelastic-like and flexoelectric mechanisms.

### Paper [85]
Title: Projecting Fermions into Exact Scar Towers: Local Protection across Phase Transitions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30143
Authors: Wenjie Wei, Kun Jiang, Hui-Ke Jin
Abstract: We construct exact scar towers in $SO(n)$ ($n\ge3$) spin chains by Gutzwiller projection of $n$-flavor flat-band Kitaev chains. The projected roots have only singlet and antisymmetric components on each bond. These local constraints allow a staggered flavor flip to generate an equidistant tower. The root and its first descendant exhaust the two-site configurations that are visited by the tower, leaving an orthogonal bond subspace unvisited. Deformations within this subspace can break $SO(n)$ symmetry and reshape the low-energy physics without altering the exact scar tower. A polarization-selective deformation drives an exact first-order transition from the symmetry-protected topological phase to a ferromagnetic phase. The tower retains its wave functions and absolute energies across the phase transition, including at the gapless point. Exact scars therefore need not inherit the symmetries, order parameters, or criticality of the ground state.

### Paper [86]
Title: A Unified Energy-Density Framework for Moir\'e Interface Energetics and Mechanics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30152
Authors: Zhangke Lei, Weidong Yan, Zhicheng Feng, Wengen Ouyang
Abstract: The mechanical response of moir\'e interfaces depends on how atomic registry combines with contact size and boundary geometry. An analytical description must retain this discrete lattice information while connecting microscopic interactions to the energetics of finite contacts. Here, we develop a unified energy-density framework for rigid van der Waals interfaces by expressing registry-dependent pair potentials and atomic densities in reciprocal space. The resulting formulation accommodates different lattice bases and atomic species, linking single-atom and molecular adsorption to infinite bilayers and finite crystalline contacts. Explicit expressions for circular and regular polygonal flakes separate the area contribution from boundary-dependent terms and connect microscopic corrugation coefficients to energy barriers, rotational torques, and interlayer shear stiffnesses. The boundary analysis recovers the quarter-power area scaling of the barrier envelope for circular flakes and distinguishes bounded oscillations from square-root area scaling for polygonal contacts according to edge orientation. A dimensionless criterion characterizes the polygon-to-circle crossover in terms of moir\'e wavelength, flake size, and side number. Direct-summation benchmarks support the finite-domain expressions, while calculations for two-dimensional ice on graphene demonstrate the corresponding boundary-scaling trends in a molecular lattice. Applications to interlayer shear and graphite compression connect the same microscopic interaction description to tangential and normal mechanical responses. The framework establishes an analytical connection between microscopic registry, moir\'e periodicity, and finite-domain geometry, providing a basis for understanding and tailoring the energetics and mechanics of van der Waals contacts.

### Paper [87]
Title: Generation and non-thermal annealing of dislocations and atomic-scale roughness by nonlinear surface acoustic waves
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30160
Authors: Yuan Xu, Maxim V. Shugaev, Leonid V. Zhigilei
Abstract: Surface acoustic waves (SAWs) are widely used to probe near-surface material properties and microstructure, but their potential to drive permanent structural modification remains largely unexplored. Large-scale molecular dynamics simulations reveal the mechanisms of defect generation and evolution induced by nonlinear Rayleigh waves in Pt. Strong crystallographic dependence of the nonlinear evolution is observed. SAWs on Pt(111) undergo rapid nonlinear sharpening and shock formation, with localized elastic energy and increasing near-surface stresses, whereas much weaker sharpening on Pt(001) allows dissipation to compete effectively with shock formation. For sufficiently strong SAWs on Pt(111), Shockley partial dislocations are emitted from the surface when the resolved shear stress approaches the theoretical shear strength. The highly localized and rapidly changing stress field at the shock front activates multiple slip systems and promotes crossslip and reactions between Shockley partials, producing complex dislocation structures with sessile stair-rod segments. Subsequent SAW interactions activate dislocation sources on slip systems where the stress is insufficient for homogeneous nucleation, leading, in one case, to sequential emission of Shockley partials and formation of a subsurface twinned domain. Repeated SAW passages progressively reduce dislocation density, demonstrating SAW-driven nonthermal annealing. Dislocation interactions with the free surface generate atomic-scale steps, terraces, and other under-coordinated configurations, providing a microscopic pathway for permanent surface modification that may contribute to the acoustically enhanced catalytic activity observed on Pt surfaces. These results establish nonlinear SAWs as drivers of nonthermal defect generation, restructuring, and annealing, opening opportunities for acoustic processing of near-surface microstructure.

### Paper [88]
Title: Quantum Geometry Effects in Lattice Dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30210
Authors: Haoshu Li, Hao Chen, Qian Niu
Abstract: Quantum geometric effects such as the Berry curvature and the quantum metric are well established in electronic transport, whereas in lattice dynamics the Berry curvature also plays a key role: adiabatically following the lattice, electrons generate a molecular Berry curvature that splits degenerate optical phonons into chiral partners. To determine how the quantum metric affects phonons, we go beyond adiabatic lattice dynamics, identifying Berry curvature and quantum metric contributions to the electronic response of the lattice. Using first-principles calculations in the magnetic topological semimetal EuB$_6$, we find that phonon modes with chiral splitting and those with nonchiral splitting coexist. Unlike the former, which originate from the Berry curvature, the latter arise from the pure quantum metric, while the Berry curvature contribution is forbidden by crystal symmetry.

### Paper [89]
Title: Dynamical evolution and rheological behavior of a sheared compound vesicle with a highly viscous core: a mesoscale particle-based study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30232
Authors: A. Lamura
Abstract: We study numerically the dynamics and the rheology of a two-dimensional compound vesicle in wall-bounded shear flow, as a minimal model of a nucleated cell. We consider the configuration in which the annular fluid is iso-viscous with the external one, while the inner core is more viscous. Increasing the core viscosity drives the external vesicle away from steady tank-treading at values $\phi \geq 0.6$. When an internal body is present, the annular fluid partially shields the outer membrane from the viscous core, so that at small filling fractions the external vesicle retains tank-treading with a higher inclination angle and the intrinsic viscosity stays comparatively low. The internal vesicle, by contrast, tumbles and migrates away from the center, and at high $\phi$ this motion couples back to the host, producing intermittent shape excursions of the external membrane. The inner viscous body thus acts as an internal governor of the dynamical state.

### Paper [90]
Title: Growing Knot Embeddings into Spherical Graphs
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30245
Authors: Eren G\"ulmez, Joost de Graaf
Abstract: Knotted structures occur throughout soft matter and biology, where topological constraints can shape relaxation and spatial organization. Here, we introduce a computational construction that maps a specific 3D knot embedding to a graph embedded on the sphere through growth and interfacial-energy relaxation. For standard 3D embeddings of prime knots with at most seven crossings, we obtain a family of growth-based graphs that generally differ from the corresponding Tait graphs. We further identify an empirical growth-graph reconstruction rule based on polygonal regions with alternating crossing structure in a knot diagram. Non-minimal diagrams demonstrate that these graphs are not knot invariants, while selected connected sums suggest that graph features associated with their prime factors can remain recognizable. Determining how these graphs transform under isotopy may reveal invariant features, which we leave for future study.

### Paper [91]
Title: Apparent thermal conductivity governed by interface-injected phonon spectra
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30276
Authors: D. G. Jeong, Terumasa Tadano, J. S. Lee
Abstract: Thermoreflectance techniques such as time- and frequency-domain thermoreflectance (TDTR and FDTR) are widely used to measure the thermal conductivity of bulk materials, thin films, and interfaces. When phonon mean free paths $\Lambda_\omega$ become comparable to the thermal penetration depth, a Fourier analysis yields an apparent thermal conductivity $\kappa_{\rm app}$ that deviates from the bulk value $\kappa_{\rm bulk}$. Recent studies have highlighted the role of the phonon spectrum injected through the transducer interface, which is generally out of equilibrium owing to frequency-dependent transmission, and have shown that accounting for this nonequilibrium distribution can yield $\kappa_{\rm app}>\kappa_{\rm bulk}$, unlike conventional suppression-function analyses that predict only $\kappa_{\rm app}\leq\kappa_{\rm bulk}$. However, the spectral characteristics governing whether $\kappa_{\rm app}$ is enhanced or reduced remain unclear. Here, we resolve the surface-temperature response of semi-infinite Si mode by mode and show that short-$\Lambda_\omega$-weighted injection raises the surface temperature, whereas long-$\Lambda_\omega$-weighted injection lowers it. This behavior is governed by how deep the injected phonons travel before thermalizing with the lattice, which is set by their mean free paths, and by how effectively the substrate conducts the injected energy away, which is limited by its long-mean-free-path phonons. For an Al (70 nm)/Si structure, Fourier fits of synthetic TDTR phase signals give $\kappa_{\rm app}$ of Si from about 93 to 360 W/(m K) for different interfacial transmission spectra, and $\kappa_{\rm app}$ correlates with the heat-flux-weighted $\Lambda_\omega$ of the transmitted phonons. Thus, $\kappa_{\rm app}$ from thermoreflectance reflects not only the substrate thermal properties but also the phonon spectrum injected across the transducer interface.

### Paper [92]
Title: Anatomy of orbital and spin responses in GaAs and Si: Hall, inverse Faraday, and magneto-optical Kerr effects
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30297
Authors: Sanaz Alikhah, Daegeun Jo, Peter M. Oppeneer
Abstract: Magneto-optical signals in nonmagnetic semiconductors entangle spin and orbital contributions, both in angular-momentum generation and magneto-optical detection. We calculate these contributions for electron-doped GaAs and Si using density-functional theory, linear-response theory, and a second-order density-matrix treatment of the inverse Faraday effect. At small electron doping, the intrinsic orbital Hall conductivity exceeds its spin counterpart in magnitude, while the orbital contribution to light-induced magnetization dominates over broad photon-energy ranges. We show that orbital- and spin-resolved optical Hall and polar Kerr coefficients exhibit stronger sensitivity to orbital moments than to equal spin moments. This contrast reflects direct electric-dipole coupling to orbital motion versus spin-orbit-mediated coupling to spin polarization. Consequently, Si maintains a substantial orbital magneto-optical response despite its weak spin-orbit interaction. At probe energies of 1.5 eV in GaAs and 3.0 eV in Si, the magnitude of the Kerr rotation per unit orbital moment is approximately 5 and 13 times larger than that per unit spin moment, respectively. Combining the calculated Hall conductivities and Kerr coefficients with a phenomenological drift-diffusion model yields predominantly orbital edge signals for the assumed diffusion constants and relaxation lengths. These results indicate that optical detection of induced magnetization in doped semiconductors such as GaAs and Si can be dominated by orbital signals, and therefore cannot be interpreted solely in terms of spin polarization.

### Paper [93]
Title: Spin-controlled thermophase and noise-optimized thermometry in multiorbital quantum dots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30315
Authors: Markus Plautz, Jeroen Danon, Jacob Linder
Abstract: Converting temperature differences into electrical signals underlies both thermoelectric energy conversion and thermometry, which is essential for managing heat in cryogenic quantum technologies. Superconducting circuits typically show a weak thermoelectric response, since their quasiparticle spectrum is nearly particle-hole symmetric. In a Josephson junction, a temperature difference can instead be converted into a superconducting phase difference in an open circuit. We investigate this thermophase effect in a setup where the junction consists of a two-orbital quantum dot with Rashba spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and Zeeman splitting. A second orbital near the superconducting coherence peaks supports quasiparticle transport over a broad gate range. Zeeman splitting can enhance the thermophase by suppressing pair transport, while spin-orbit hybridization shifts the orbital resonances and can reverse the sign of the response. We assess the junction as a phase-based thermometer using a noise budget that includes readout and current noise together with fluctuations of the orbital energies and the magnetic field. The optimal operating points do not maximize the thermophase but balance a large quasiparticle response against sensitivity to parameter fluctuations. Under experimentally motivated noise assumptions, we obtain projected noise-equivalent temperatures of order $10\,\mu\mathrm{K}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ and determine the range over which a linear calibration remains accurate. Gate-tunable quantum-dot junctions thus offer a phase-based route to sensing temperature differences in superconducting circuits at sub-kelvin temperatures.

### Paper [94]
Title: Atomistic insight into defect-mediated boron redistribution and high-temperature strengthening in TiAl alloys
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30330
Authors: Xiang Meng, Shuhei Shinzato, Zhiqiang Zhao, Linfeng Yu, Jian Guo, Liangli Chu, Kepeng Ouyang, Shigenobu Ogata, Zhuhua Zhang
Abstract: Boron (B) microalloying can effectively refine microstructures of as-cast TiAl alloys and regulate their mechanical properties. Yet, how dilute B exists in TiAl and interacts with defects and how these interactions govern high-temperature deformation remain elusive. Here, we systematically elucidate defect-mediated B redistribution in TiAl alloys and its consequences for their deformation using hybrid grand-canonical Monte Carlo/molecular dynamics simulations augmented with a newly developed high-fidelity machine-learning potential. As a typical product of B microalloying, borides are markedly stiffer than the TiAl matrix and act as brittle reinforcing phases, while borides/matrix interfacial adhesion depends on surface termination and stacking sequence. In the bulk, interstitial B tends to be trapped at Ti-rich octahedral sites by high migration barriers but can kinetically evolve into substitutional dopants upon approaching Al vacancies. Increased tensile stress and vacancy concentration further promote B enrichment in the bulk. B is enriched at semi-coherent $\gamma/\alpha_2$ boundaries and preferentially partitions into the $\alpha_2$ phase. In polycrystalline TiAl, B accumulates primarily in disordered regions, raising the average flow stress by 9.4% at 1 at.% B and 900 K. This strengthening is accompanied by reduced shear strain accumulation. Simulated microstructural evolution and stacking-fault energetics indicate that B impedes dislocation glide via local pinning and suppresses deformation twinning, thereby increasing resistance to plastic deformation. By linking solute redistribution to defect interactions and deformation, these findings establish an atomistic framework for understanding and designing high-performance alloys based on interstitial solutes.

### Paper [95]
Title: Edge-asymmetric magnetism in semi-Dirac quantum dots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30343
Authors: Mustafa Polat
Abstract: In this study, we investigated the electronic and magnetic properties of quantum dots (QDs) throughout the transition from the Dirac limit to the semi-Dirac limit and beyond. When non-interacting semi-Dirac fermions are confined in triangular QDs, the resulting electronic states near the Fermi level are localized asymmetrically among the three edges of these systems. In the presence of electron interactions, we show that these low-energy states favor the formation of edge-asymmetric magnetism in triangular QDs. In particular, the magnetic moments were localized only along a single edge of the zigzag triangular QDs, corresponding to an edge-asymmetric ferromagnetic ground state. For various system sizes, these zigzag triangular QDs exhibit a one-unit increase in the ground-state total spin ($\Delta S_z = +1$) beyond the semi-Dirac limit, revealing a striking result of this transition. In contrast, the remaining two edges are antiferromagnetically coupled in the armchair triangular QDs, indicating an edge-asymmetric antiferromagnetic (AFM) ground state. We further identified the AFM ground states in hexagonal QDs, indicating that these types of asymmetric configurations are not unique to triangular QDs. Collectively, edge-asymmetric magnetism could create potential opportunities for future spintronics.

### Paper [96]
Title: High-Throughput Screening of Phononic Bandgap in Experimentally Realized Materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30344
Authors: Mohammad Bagheri, Kameyab Raza Abidi, Pekka Koskinen
Abstract: Materials with a phonon band gap offer a unique mechanism to restrict phonon scattering, making them promising candidates for developing functional materials for targeted thermal applications. Unlocking this mechanism across broad material classes requires systematic exploration, but first-principles methods and experiments are too expensive. Here, we used the universal machine-learning interatomic potential to quantify phonons and thermodynamic properties. We validated the accuracy of machine-learning-calculated results by comparing them with density-functional theory. We screened 154,879 materials and performed phonon calculations for 42,347 thermodynamically stable and experimentally observed compounds. We then performed phonon band gap analysis for dynamically stable materials and identified 11,176 materials with a phononic gap, including 10,960 materials with optical-optical gaps and 270 with acoustic-optical gaps. We also analyzed the lattice thermal conductivity of materials with phonon band gaps to identify candidates with acoustic-optical gaps and low thermal conductivity. Finally, by solving the linearized phonon Boltzmann equation from first principles, we demonstrate low room-temperature lattice thermal conductivities in three Li-metal binaries.

### Paper [97]
Title: Strain-Induced Tetragonal-to-Cubic Transition in MAPbI$_3$ from Machine-Learned Molecular Dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30348
Authors: Daan Holleman, Luuk Klein Nagelvoort, Monica Morales-Masis, Linn Leppert
Abstract: Metal-halide perovskites are promising photovoltaic absorbers whose optoelectronic properties are highly tunable. Epitaxial strain offers a route to stabilize desirable phases, tune band gaps, and reduce energetic disorder, but rational strain engineering requires predictive models. Static, zero-temperature "strained-bulk" density functional theory (DFT) calculations, successful for oxide perovskites, neglect the dynamic disorder and anharmonicity that govern halide perovskites at ambient temperature. Here, we show that molecular dynamics (MD) based on machine-learned force fields trained on-the-fly on DFT reproduces the structural and optoelectronic response of methylammonium (MA) lead iodide (MAPbI$_3$) to epitaxial strain at 300 K. We simulate bulk and free-standing slab models across experimentally accessible biaxial strains by locking the in-plane lattice vectors in nanosecond-long simulations. We predict a tetragonal-to-cubic transition at $\sim$0.5% tensile strain, marked by $c/a = 1$ and the loss of the $a^0a^0c^-$ tilting pattern, in agreement with experiments on epitaxial MAPbI$_3$ films on KCl. MA cations retain preferential orientations in the strain-induced cubic phase, unlike in its temperature-induced counterpart. Bulk and slab models yield consistent trends, with surface effects decaying within a few octahedral layers. Band gaps computed as ensemble averages over MD snapshots blue-shift under tensile strain, consistent with experiment, whereas static calculations on averaged structures predict a red shift. Our study positions MD with constrained lattice vectors as a method for predicting finite-temperature strain phase diagrams and guiding the epitaxial design of perovskite absorbers.

### Paper [98]
Title: Manipulation of Ferroelectric Polarization Under Near-Gap Light
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30359
Authors: Hanli Cui, Jian Zhou
Abstract: Noncontacting light control of ferroelectric (FE) order has been receiving extensive interest in condensed matter physics. In this work, we investigate near-resonant light irradiation that induces structure-dependent photoexcited carrier density along the transition pathway. We combine a minimal Rice-Mele model containing intersite density-density correlations with ab initio calculations for realistic materials such as GeTe and SnTe. If the photon energy is below-gap without carrier excitation, only off-resonant scattering occurs and the FE order tends to be diminished. Slightly increasing photon energy at the near-gap regime would lead to a centrosymmetric non-polar (NP) to FE phase transition, which is not thermally accessible in conventional pressure-temperature phase diagram. This is in contrast to the well-above-gap light situation that tends to suppress the FE order under a fixed amount of photoexcitation. We apply phenomenological Landau theory and show that they belong to the continuous and first-order phase transitions, respectively. The occupation constrained calculations suggest that these reversible NP-FE phase transitions could be triggered under moderate light fluence. Our mechanism provides a potential route to reversible light-stabilized polar phases with reduced lattice heating and marginal lattice damages.

### Paper [99]
Title: Critical Topology: A New Perspective for Phase Transitions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30368
Authors: Xue-Jia Yu
Abstract: Critical topology has emerged as a form of quantum criticality beyond the Landau paradigm, challenging conventional approaches to classifying universality classes by introducing topological properties as an additional criterion. Despite substantial progress over the past five years, several fundamental questions remain open. Theoretically, can we establish a general criterion for the emergence of critical topology? Can a unified theoretical framework be extended beyond one-dimensional quantum many-body systems? What efficient numerical methods can be developed to investigate critical topological states in higher-dimensional strongly correlated systems? Experimentally, research has so far focused primarily on single-particle critical topology. An important next step is to realize and characterize many-body critical topology in quantum systems using platforms such as Rydberg atom arrays, superconducting quantum processors, and quantum materials.
  Major advances in statistical physics often arise from re-examining fundamental concepts. The theory of critical phenomena developed by Landau, Ginzburg, and Wilson, together with the topological phase transitions pioneered by Kosterlitz and Thouless, has now inspired new discoveries in artificial acoustic metamaterials. These experimental observations mark a beginning rather than an endpoint for research on critical topology, and further advances in this direction are anticipated.

### Paper [100]
Title: Gyroidal Magnetic Order with Weyl Magnons
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30374
Authors: Kiyu Fukui, Yukitoshi Motome
Abstract: The gyroid, a triply periodic minimal surface found widely in nature and materials, has remained largely unexplored as a design principle for magnetic order. We introduce gyroidal magnetic order on the hyperoctagon lattice, the skeletal network of the gyroid, in which each magnetic moment points along the local $C_3$ axis that coincides with the local normal to the gyroid surface. The resulting noncoplanar spin texture constitutes a direct magnetic imprint of the gyroid geometry. We show that this order can be stabilized by single-ion anisotropy, the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, or the symmetric off-diagonal $\Gamma$ interaction combined with the Kitaev interaction, and remains robust against quantum fluctuations. Its magnon spectrum hosts symmetry-enforced pseudospin-1 and pseudospin-1/2 Weyl points that, unlike their electronic counterparts, persist under symmetry-allowed perturbations. Our results establish the gyroid as a geometric platform for magnetic order and symmetry-enforced topological magnon excitations.

### Paper [101]
Title: Neutron Spin Resonance as Probe of Triplet Pair Density Waves in CeCoIn$_5$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30395
Authors: David W. Tam, Ilaria Maccari, St\'ephane Raymond, Barry Winn, Paul Steffens, Arno Hiess, Jacques Ollivier, Dariusz Jakub Gawryluk, Ekaterina Pomjakushina, Aline Ramires, Michel Kenzelmann
Abstract: Spin resonance modes are among the most characteristic collective excitations of unconventional superconductors, yet their microscopic origin remains unresolved. Although commonly interpreted as purely magnetic excitations, this picture does not explain the rich magnetic field evolution of these modes observed in heavy-fermion superconductors. Here we combine inelastic neutron scattering and symmetry analysis to investigate the evolution of the spin resonance modes in the heavy-fermion superconductor CeCoIn$_5$ under an in-plane magnetic field. At high fields, approaching the upper critical field of the superconductor, we discover a third resonance mode with relatively high intensity. We demonstrate that the resonance branches observed experimentally can be interpreted as collective modes of the superconducting condensate involving transitions to excited superconducting states. This framework provides a unified interpretation of the field-induced splitting of the resonance, the newly observed mode at high fields, the relative neutron scattering intensities of each mode, and provides the first direct evidence that a spin resonance mode can be associated with a singlet-triplet excitation of a superconductor.

### Paper [102]
Title: Universal thermodynamics and BKT transition of the planar Bose gas from weak to strong interactions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30407
Authors: Yaniss Rabahi, Ivan Balog, Adam Ran\c{c}on
Abstract: We compute the universal thermodynamics of the dilute two-dimensional Bose gas at weak and strong coupling using the functional renormalization group (FRG). We employ an approximation that retains the full field dependence of the effective potential and spatial derivative terms, extending previous FRG calculations. We find that the equation of state is remarkably insensitive to the approximation scheme, with differences of only a few percent even at strong coupling. By contrast, the improved approximation substantially improves the description of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition, reproducing the universal jump of the superfluid density and the critical anomalous dimension to within a few percent. At weak coupling, our results agree very well with classical-field Monte Carlo simulations, while at stronger coupling we observe deviations from classical-field scaling, valid at weak coupling. Our approach thus provides a quantitative description of the thermodynamics and superfluid density across the BKT transition, including in the strong-coupling regime where classical-field methods are no longer reliable.

### Paper [103]
Title: Scanning nanowire quantum dot with radio-frequency read-out
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30427
Authors: Aur\`ele Kamber, Luca Forrer, Valentina Zannier, Fabio Beltram, Lucia Sorba, Martino Poggio, Floris Braakman
Abstract: Scanning single-electron transistors (SETs) are highly sensitive local probes of charge and electric potential, but their integration with robust height control for scanning probe microscopy remains challenging. Furthermore, readout bandwidths are limited to tens of kHz, while characterizing faster charge dynamics is often of interest. Here, we present a scanning SET in the form of a nanowire (NW) quantum dot (QD) integrated at the apex of a non-contact atomic force microscopy (NC-AFM) cantilever. We demonstrate NC-AFM topographic imaging and local electrometry capability by mapping the electrostatic potential generated by a set of nanoscale voltage-biased gate electrodes. Comparison with electrostatic simulations shows agreement with the measured potential profiles. We demonstrate radio-frequency (rf) reflectometry read-out of the sensor, by embedding the QD in an LC-resonator. This platform establishes a route towards high-bandwidth scanning electrometry with simultaneous topographic contrast.

### Paper [104]
Title: Exact solution of the long-range voter model in arbitrary dimensions: correlations and effective-dimensional scaling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30474
Authors: Fabrizio Rippa, Jacopo Alexander Garofalo, Giuseppe Petrillo, Eugenio Lippiello
Abstract: Long-range interactions can profoundly alter collective ordering and critical behavior, yet exact ana- lytical results for non-equilibrium systems with such interactions remain scarce. We obtain an exact analytical solution for the spatial correlation function of the voter model with long-range interac- tions in arbitrary spatial dimension. Starting from the evolution equation for the Fourier modes, we identify the long-wavelength dispersion generated by the interaction kernel and derive the resulting coarsening and stationary scaling regimes. The solution yields the critical condition separating these regimes, the characteristic coarsening length, and the asymptotic decay of stationary correlations. These results establish an exact effective-dimensional correspondence: the long-range model in dimen- sion d, with interaction exponent \b{eta}, exhibits the same scaling exponents as a short-range voter model in the effective dimension deff = 2d/\b{eta}. The correspondence holds across the coarsening, marginal, and stationary regimes, while the associated scaling functions need not coincide

### Paper [105]
Title: Collective order from language models without communication, memory or shared context
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30521
Authors: Yong Wang
Abstract: Physical matter orders without a centre: units interact only with neighbours, and fluctuations carry them over energy barriers from a locally stable arrangement to the ordered state. We ask whether language-model agents can do the same, treating each memoryless query as a particle that sees its neighbours but cannot communicate, has no identifier or shared context and receives the same question as all others. Such a particle behaves as if at zero temperature: told a rule or a temperature, it returns the most probable state, and a collective can freeze in a local minimum. We therefore give each particle only a goal and a die whose weights it sets itself, and test several models. Some of them use the die as a noise source even at a local optimum, with weights that fall with the energy cost of the move; on a lattice the energy of one of them climbs over barriers and reaches the ground state, where the noise stops by itself. Particles in three-lane counterflow also order, from mixed traffic into one-way lanes: a spontaneous symmetry breaking. Decentralized collective order can thus arise from disorder without communication, memory or shared context, with noise that the particles choose themselves.

### Paper [106]
Title: Nonequilibrium Dynamics of R-loop Formation in the CRISPR-Cas9 System
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30527
Authors: Yu Liu, Bing Miao
Abstract: The binding specificity of CRISPR-Cas9 is governed by the stepwise formation of an R-loop, in which the sgRNA displaces the nontarget DNA strand and hybridizes with the target strand in discrete base pair steps. Here we develop a nonequilibrium dynamics framework that models R-loop formation as a continuous-time Markov birth-death process over twenty base pairs. Each transition rate is derived from Eyring transition-state theory and the Bell model, and combines three physical contributions, i.e., the torsional strain generated by the B-form to A-form helical conversion at the unwinding front, a base stacking correction that couples neighboring base pairs, and the allosteric work supplied by Cas9 conformational changes. We further derive the entropy production of the process, which is decomposed into activation, stacking, torsional, and allosteric terms. The resulting prediction model uses no external thermodynamic database and reaches Spearman correlations of 0.84 and 0.82 in two independent libraries of dCas9-sgRNA mismatch variants measured in vivo. The mismatch-type dependence is carried by one twist deviation and one pairing weight per type. Our model recovers the seed region sensitivity, the PAM-proximal activity gradient, and the moderate cost of dDrG mismatches, while isolating the high-activity double mismatch tail as the regime in which the present free-energy mapping is inadequate. The framework gives quantitative predictions on how DNA supercoiling and sequence context modulate binding affinity, and provides a mechanistic basis for sgRNA design.

### Paper [107]
