The same geometry also exhibits clear signatures of the tomographic regime, potentially allowing for a quantitative discrimination between viscous and tomographic flow in experiments. Our results demonstrate that multiterminal devices are a simpler experimental route to characterize transport regimes in electron liquids, relative to space-resolved imaging experiments.

### Paper [214]
Title: Bootstrapping ground state properties of classical frustrated magnets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06784
Authors: Nisarga Paul, Gil Refael
Abstract: We introduce a method based on semidefinite programming that produces rigorous two-sided bounds on ground state energy densities and correlation functions of translation-invariant classical spin models on infinite lattices. In this method, the challenge of non-convex optimization on an infinite lattice is replaced with a hierarchy of finite-size convex optimizations arising from positivity conditions that any probability distribution over spin configurations must satisfy. This adapts the Lasserre hierarchy in the theory of polynomial optimization to the context of frustrated magnetism, and we prove convergence of this hierarchy in the thermodynamic limit. Our method subsumes the Luttinger--Tisza method and further applies to non-quadratic Hamiltonians and non-Bravais lattices, thus addressing limitations of prior analytical methods. We apply the method to various two-dimensional frustrated spin models, where it brackets the energy densities and observables accurately across large parameter ranges with typical run times of seconds per parameter point.

### Paper [215]
Title: Adaptive Slater Koster Parameters: Crossing Oxidation States with Density Functional Tight Binding
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19977
Authors: Yihua Song, Artem Samtsevych, Anton Beiersdorfer, Tobias Melson, Christoph Scheurer, Karsten Reuter, Chiara Panosetti
Abstract: We propose to adapt the confined pseudo-atomic orbitals underpinning the Slater-Koster (SK) tables in density functional tight binding (DFTB) to local atomic environments. Assigning optimal SK parameters to metal atoms in different oxidation states improves the electronic structure of a partially oxidized Ni surface and, with adapted repulsion, Li insertion energetics in graphite. Their smooth, interpretable response to chemical and spatial environment, independently observed for Ni and Co oxides, enables a simple, physically informed machine-learning scheme, here applied to Ni-O binaries.

### Paper [216]
Title: Properties of the skyrmion crystal in the Heisenberg triangular lattice with scalar chirality
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21010
Authors: H. Bocquet, C. J. Ganahl, M. Scheurer, P. M. Derlet, A. M. L\"auchli
Abstract: Skyrmion crystals have been primarily discovered under a magnetic field for materials with noncentrosymmetric interactions. More recent developments have investigated the stability of skyrmion crystals in itinerant magnets without a magnetic field. In this study, we find that a type of skyrmion crystal with two topological charges per unit cell and no magnetization at the ferromagnetic point in reciprocal space, SkX-2, is naturally stabilized in an $\mathrm{SO}(3)$-symmetric model with short-range interactions realized by the Heisenberg model on the triangular lattice with scalar chirality. We complement our numerical results with a theoretical analysis that quantitatively describes the transition from the ferromagnetic to the SkX-2 ground state and the evolution of the topological charge density. Despite the constraints given by the Mermin-Wagner theorem at finite temperature, the SkX-2 can either exhibit a first-order phase transition with translation symmetry breaking, or a continuous transition to a floating solid depending on the charge density, which is given by the strength of the scalar chirality interaction. Finally, the numerical results indicate the presence of $\mathbb{Z}_2$-vortices at finite temperature above the tetrahedral ground state in the antiferromagnetic region of our model, suggesting the existence of a vortex topological transition similar to that reported in the ordering of the pure antiferromagnetic model.

### Paper [217]
Title: Symmetry-Protected Bipartite Entanglement in Hopf-Linked Quantum Rings
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26622
Authors: V. Yogesh, Prosenjit Maity
Abstract: We study two interacting electrons in Hopf-linked quantum rings, one electron on each ring. In the absence of the inter-ring interaction, the two lowest orbital states on each ring have the same energy. For the ideal geometry, the Fourier coefficients of the three-dimensional Coulomb kernel satisfy $V_{1,0}=-V_{0,1}$, making the positive-parity block of the four-state Hamiltonian diagonal in the Bell basis. Unitary symmetries of the full orbital Hamiltonian preserve the parity sectors under virtual transitions. For pure states odd under ring exchange followed by a half-turn on each ring ($\mathcal Y=-1$), the Schmidt weights occur in equal pairs, giving at least one bit of entanglement between the rings. An elliptic deformation changes the Coulomb selection rule to first order while preserving the link. Finite-basis diagonalization shows that higher orbitals can reduce the probability of obtaining the encoded Bell pair by projection onto the two lowest orbitals of each ring even when the entanglement between the rings remains close to one bit.

### Paper [218]
Title: Giant dielectric permittivity in Nb-doped rutile crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02072
Authors: D. Nuzhnyy, V. Bovtun, J. Petzelt, M. Savinov, M. Kempa, P. Levinsk\'y, K. Beranov\'a, P. Van\v{e}k, T. Kmje\v{c}, T. Ostapchuk, P. Ku\v{z}el, J. Hlinka, D. Crandles, M. Cosco, Y. Hashimoto, H. Taniguchi, S. Kamba
Abstract: Dielectric properties of Nb-doped (~1.5 at.%) rutile single crystals were studied in the 10-300 K temperature range (at frequencies below the MHz range down to 0.3 K) in a broad frequency range, including terahertz and infrared range, to understand the origin of its giant permittivity. The results were fitted, modelled and compared with those of the undoped rutile crystal measured in the terahertz and infrared ranges. The primary effect originates from the near-electrode depletion layer of lower conductivity compared to the bulk (surface barrier-layer capacitor effect), which causes a strong thermally activated relaxation in the MHz dielectric spectra. In the higher frequency range, the main difference between doped and undoped crystals is the presence of an overdamped microwave excitation (central mode) in the doped crystal for both polarizations, persisting down to 10 K and not thermally activated. This accounts for the previously reported increased permittivity values down to 2 K - where all lower-frequency relaxations are frozen - compared to undoped crystals. It also explains why our low-frequency permittivity at 0.3 K exceeds its THz value. In similar Nb-doped rutile single crystals it was quite recently ascribed [V. A. Dinh et al., Phys. Rev. B 114, 024112, (2026)] to microwave tunneling of molecular polarons. Polar phonons are affected only by a slightly increased damping.

### Paper [219]
Title: Thermodynamic Uncertainty Relation For a Multi-Phase Alternating Renewal Random Walk
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16339
Authors: Fatemeh Karimi, Farhad H. Jafarpour
Abstract: Thermodynamic Uncertainty Relations (TURs) impose universal bounds linking current precision to entropy production in nonequilibrium systems. While general bounds like the Proesmans--Van den Broeck (PV) relation provide a broad framework, they often remain loose for processes characterized by renewal events. In this work, we derive a generalized entropic bound for current fluctuations in renewal-reward processes. By utilizing a rigorous variance decomposition within the framework of renewal-reward theory, we obtain a model-independent bound that is rigorous and, for a range of parameters in the examples considered, tighter than the PV relation evaluated with the total entropy production of the extended (position and heading) state space. Notably, in the linear-response regime, our bound correctly scales with the renewal rate and identifies a precision penalty arising from fluctuations of the cycle reward. These results provide a physically informative constraint on the precision of run-and-tumble-type dynamics and highlight the universal limits of transport in complex stochastic walkers.

### Paper [220]
Title: Fate of odd-parity magnetism in one dimension
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26222
Authors: Kasper Rettedal Eikeland, Sondre Duna Lundemo, Asle Sudb{\o}
Abstract: We consider a one-dimensional model for a $p$-wave magnet within the bosonization framework. The model consists of itinerant electrons described by an extended Hubbard model coupled to a chain of localized moments through the Kondo exchange. The classical ground state of the local-moment system captures the salient features of an odd-parity magnet. By bosonizing the coupled system, a description in terms of coupled Luttinger liquids follows, giving rise to a rich weak-coupling phase diagram. It is shown that the spin chain develops quasi long-range order consistent with the combined time-reversal and translation symmetry defining the $p$-wave magnet. We highlight the peculiar role played by this order in establishing a commensurability condition on the electronic filling under which additional interactions appear in the bosonic field theory. It is demonstrated that these interactions endow the electron spectral function with a pronounced $p$-wave character. Away from commensurate filling, these interactions are rendered irrelevant and the ensuing $p$-wave character is lost.

### Paper [221]
Title: Bridging electrode preparation and electrocatalyst performance with physics-based causal AI using Bayesian Structural Causal Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30898
Authors: Evelyna Wang, Linda Hung, Sam Witty, Michaela Burke Stevens, Kevin Tran
Abstract: State-of-the-art artificial intelligence (AI) and Machine-Learning (ML) tools have not yet enabled rapid design of next-generation materials. Detailed physical understanding of how material properties affect device performance is required to advance materials development. For example, optimization of ink parameters for electrocatalysts has no known physical mathematical model and thus insights are difficult to translate from material studies to device studies. Herein, we demonstrate how to use the emerging AI tool, physics-based structural causal models (SCMs), to extract quantitative causative insights from complex heterogeneous electrochemical systems with small (n < 10), but multi-modal datasets. Our SCM quantitatively separates the role that varying the support-to-catalyst ratios and total material loadings plays on catalytic performance. The proof of concept model developed in this work enables root-cause-analysis on the cyclic voltammograms of manganese-antimony oxide oxygen reduction electrocatalysts on Vulcan carbon supports tested in alkaline media using a rotating disc electrode device configuration. Our computational causal analyses quantitatively disentangle how the catalyst performance is affected by the number of active sites versus the thickness of the electrode. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of SCMs with physical equations applied to electrochemical materials and their performance.

### Paper [222]
Title: Connecting Microseismicity to Lithology via a Model of Slip Avalanches
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06886
Authors: Ethan A. Mullen (Department of Physics and Anthony J. Leggett Institute for Condensed Matter Theory, University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, Urbana, USA), Yanbo Wang (Integrated Multi-Physics Lab, Department of Mechanical Engineering, The University of Utah, Salt Lake City, USA), Jordan J. Sickle (Department of Physics and Anthony J. Leggett Institute for Condensed Matter Theory, University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, Urbana, USA), Frank W. DelRio (Material, Physical, and Chemical Sciences Center, Sandia National Laboratories, Albuquerque, USA), Pania Newell (Integrated Multi-Physics Lab, Department of Mechanical Engineering, The University of Utah, Salt Lake City, USA), Anastasia G. Ilgen (Geothermal Research Department, Sandia National Laboratories, Albuquerque, USA), Karin A. Dahmen (Department of Physics and Anthony J. Leggett Institute for Condensed Matter Theory, University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, Urbana, USA)
Abstract: Fluid injection into the earth's crust can induce small and frequent earthquakes in the subsurface. Predicting their sizes and temporal occurrences via statistical analysis is crucial for safe operations in unconventional oil and gas recovery, enhanced geothermal systems, and geologic carbon storage. Here we show that a simple micromechanical model of slip avalanches in slowly deforming solids predicts the slip statistics observed over drastically different spatial scales, namely meter-scale microseismic observations and nanometer- to micrometer-scale nanoindentation experiments can be described with this model. Microseismic catalogs extracted from high-pressure fluid injection operations into geological basins with various lithologies and nanoindentation experiments on shale across a wide range of temperatures and mineral compositions yield statistics consistent with model predictions. This universality across materials, temperatures, and scales is consistent with the prediction that the slip statistics result from only a few basic properties. Previously debated deviations of the statistics in layered sedimentary formations are explained by finite-size and stress-integrative effects resulting from mechanically weak bedding planes. The slip statistics therefore provide important information about the structure and scales of the bedding planes. Conversely, the basin structure can also be used to predict the probability distribution for the sizes of triggered microseismic events.

### Paper [223]
Title: Universal swelling of tangentially driven ring polymers in two dimensions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09436
Authors: Antonio Lamura, Marisol Ripoll
Abstract: The interplay of tangential activity and excluded-volume interactions in ring polymers adsorbed to a surface, consistently results in the overall swelling of the ring configurations. This is in strong contrast to the case for three dimensional linear and ring polymers, where activity induces frequently collapsed structures. By means of Brownian Dynamic simulations, we investigate how the scaling properties of such active rings can be universally characterized by an activity-dependent Flory exponent, as a generalization of the equilibrium behavior. At high activity, an effective persistence length characterizes the conformations of active flexible rings.

### Paper [224]
Title: Microscopically informed constitutive framework for stress overshoot, yielding, and strain hardening in amorphous materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13734
Authors: Ankit Singh, Valeriy V. Ginzburg, Alessio Zaccone
Abstract: We develop a microscopic constitutive theory for the nonlinear deformation of metallic and polymer glasses based on nonaffine elasticity coupled to irreversible many-body relaxation. The theory predicts the full stress--strain response, from linear elasticity through stress overshoot and yielding to steady plastic flow. We show that stress overshoot originates from the competition between a nonaffine elastic instability induced by strain-driven loss of mechanical connectivity at the atomic/molecular level, and viscous dissipation associated with structural relaxation. For polymer glasses, finite chain extensibility naturally accounts for strain hardening at large deformation. The stretched-exponential relaxation exponent is obtained independently from stress or modulus relaxation measurements and provides the primary dynamical input to the theory. Using a small set of physically meaningful parameters, the model quantitatively reproduces experimental stress--strain curves for metallic glasses, polycarbonate, PMMA, and epoxy resins over a broad range of strain rates. These results establish a unified microscopic framework linking relaxation dynamics, yielding, plastic flow, and strain hardening in amorphous solids.

### Paper [225]
Title: Quantum transport in Cooper pair splitters using hierarchical equations of motion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22235
Authors: Riya Baruah, Neill Lambert, Franco Nori, Christian Flindt
Abstract: We investigate charge transport in Cooper pair splitters beyond the weak-coupling and Markovian limits. To this end, we employ hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM), which can capture the combined effects of strong coupling to the leads, nonperturbative interactions, and finite voltage and temperature differences. Within this framework, we compute the electric currents as functions of the level positions of a Cooper pair splitter for various voltage and temperature configurations. In the large-bias regime, our results reduce to analytical expressions obtained from a Markovian Lindblad equation. However, recent experiments were conducted with finite voltage or temperature differences, where a Markovian description may not suffice. In this regime, HEOM yield quantitative agreement with the measured currents. We can also account for an experimentally observed thermoelectric effect in Cooper pair splitters. Our results show that HEOM provide a useful framework for describing nonequilibrium quantum transport in Cooper pair splitters and related hybrid devices.

### Paper [226]
Title: Evolution of the electronic and superconducting properties of Re-based quinary high-entropy alloys under chemical and physical pressure
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23056
Authors: Thiagarajan Maran, Yoshiya Uwatoko, Sathea Suweatha M N, Sonika Jangid, R. P. Singh, Arumugam Sonachalam, Sathiskumar Mariappan
Abstract: We report a comparative study of chemical- and physical-pressure effects on the electronic and superconducting properties of the Re-based quinary high-entropy alloys (HEAs) [Nb0.67-xRex][TiZrHf]0.33 (x = 0.10, 0.20, and 0.56). Increasing Re concentration suppresses the superconducting transition temperature Tc from 5.4 to 3.9 K while producing positive, composition-dependent cocktail-effect ratios. Field-dependent transport and magnetization establish all three compositions as strongly type-II superconductors and identify x = 0.20 as the most distinctive composition, with the largest upper critical field and Ginzburg-Landau parameter and a Maki parameter close to unity. Most importantly, the pressure coefficient of Tc changes sign across the bcc-hcp structural change: dTc/dP is positive for the bcc x = 0.10 and 0.20 samples, with values of approximately 0.018 and 0.053 K/GPa, respectively, but negative for the hcp x = 0.56 sample, with a value of approximately -0.033 K/GPa. This systematic contrast within a single chemically related alloy series establishes a robust structure-associated superconducting response and identifies crystal structure as a key organizing variable under compression. Metallic transport and superconductivity remain robust up to approximately 8 GPa in all three compositions. These results reveal that chemical substitution and hydrostatic compression are complementary but nonequivalent routes for tuning superconductivity in Re-based HEAs.

### Paper [227]
Title: On-site interaction energies for Hubbard models with shielded polar molecules
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28533
Authors: Carlin Stewart-Wiese, Joy Dutta, Jeremy M. Hutson
Abstract: We show how to control on-site interaction energies $U$ for pairs of shielded ultracold molecules on the same lattice site. We use 2-dimensional effective potentials appropriate for microwave shielding, which have dipole-dipole character at long range but feature a very large repulsive core when the two molecules come close together. This causes very strong correlation between the motions of two molecules on the same site and produces field-linked states. We identify field-linked states for which $U$ can be tuned over a wide range, including across zero, as a function of lattice strength or shielding conditions. This will give access to previously unexplored regimes of strongly dipolar Hubbard physics with multiple site occupancy.

### Paper [228]
Title: Mechanism-Dependent Descriptors for Oxygen Capacity in Perovskite Oxides
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29014
Authors: Shuiping Gong, Yi Li, Eric Isbrandt, Xiaoman Guo, Jie Su, Zhenhao Zhou, Xiaobo Liao, Jian Deng, Jiangang He, Tao Deng, Runxia Cai, Chaochao Dun, Zhenpeng Yao
Abstract: Perovskite oxides can reversibly accommodate substantial changes in oxygen stoichiometry, making them attractive for clean-energy technologies including chemical looping and oxygen storage. However, predicting oxygen capacity across diverse compositions remains challenging. Here, we combine experiments and first-principles calculations to establish composition-capacity relationships using the model perovskite series LnxSr1-xCoO3. Increasing redox-inactive Sr2+ content promotes high-valence Co4+ formation, expanding the cationic redox reservoir and enhancing oxygen capacity. While oxygen-vacancy formation energy tracks this trend for light rare earths, net oxygen release counterintuitively decreases from La to Lu despite lower vacancy formation energies. We reveal that this behavior originates from an alternative charge-compensation pathway where lattice oxygen is partially oxidized to O1-like species. Heavy rare earths (Tb-Lu) preferentially stabilize these oxygen holes through distinct local bonding, with charge compensation involving both oxidized lattice oxygen and reduced cations, ultimately suppressing oxygen release. We identify the average metal-oxygen bond strength, quantified by the integrated crystal orbital Hamilton population (ICOHP), as a physically meaningful descriptor when anionic redox dominates. These findings reveal how distinct charge-compensation mechanisms determine the applicability of oxygen-capacity descriptors, establishing a mechanism-dependent framework for predicting and optimizing oxygen exchange in perovskite oxides.

### Paper [229]
Title: Quantum resetting with memory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02297
Authors: Gabriele de Mauro, Manas Kulkarni, Satya N. Majumdar
Abstract: We introduce a quantum stochastic resetting protocol with uniform memory, in which each resetting event returns the system to a state visited at a time chosen uniformly from its entire history. The resulting dynamics is nonunitary, non-Markovian and a direct quantum generalization of the classical preferential relocation model. Working in the energy eigenbasis, we derive the exact evolution of every density-matrix element for an arbitrary time-independent Hamiltonian and show that the Hamiltonian enters the dynamics only through the corresponding Bohr frequencies. This leads to a natural distinction between two classes of quantum systems: gapped and gapless. In \emph{gapped systems} (systems with a discrete energy spectrum), while the diagonal elements remain unchanged, the off-diagonal elements of the density matrix in the energy eigenbasis decay algebraically with a continuously varying exponent and with an amplitude that oscillates periodically in $\log t$. The system therefore approaches a stationary state that is independent of the resetting rate and retains a strong memory of the initial state. In \emph{gapless systems} (systems with a continuous energy spectrum), arbitrarily small Bohr frequencies prevent stationarity. Instead, the position distribution spreads on the universal (ultra-slow) scale $\log(rt)/r$, independently of the initial state and of the details of the Hamiltonian. We illustrate these results with a two-level system, a harmonic oscillator, and a free quantum particle, and contrast them with their classical counterparts.

### Paper [230]
Title: Analytical Floquet Quantum Statistics from Nonequilibrium Green's Functions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04558
Authors: Yuhua Ren, Gaomin Tang, Hui Pan, Jian-Sheng Wang
Abstract: We derive an analytical expression for the steady-state quantum statistics of periodically driven quantum systems coupled to a bath using the nonequilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism. By embedding Floquet theory into NEGF, we obtain closed expressions for the retarded, advanced, and lesser Green's functions in the Floquet representation, yielding the Floquet Fermi distribution in which the steady-state occupation is expressed as a weighted sum of Fermi functions evaluated at energies shifted by integer multiples of the driving frequency. The weights are determined solely by the Fourier components of the micromotion operator, providing a transparent interpretation of Floquet sideband occupations. We extend the analysis beyond diagonal commuting Hamiltonians treated previously, and derive the off-diagonal decoherences that arise under degeneracies. We further show that the robust Floquet distribution remains valid for a broad class of weakly coupled bath spectral functions beyond the ideal featureless-bath approximation. Finally, we establish a Floquet version of the Landauer formula for the DC part of the current, in which the equilibrium Fermi functions are replaced by their Floquet-modified counterparts. Together, these results provide a coherent description of Floquet quantum statistics and transport in periodically driven open quantum systems.

### Paper [231]
Title: Topological Charge-Transfer Excitons
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05308
Authors: Huiyuan Zheng, Kaijie Yang, Ting Cao, Di Xiao
Abstract: Excitons possess internal structure absent from single-particle Bloch particles, allowing their band topology to emerge from the bound-state structure rather than being inherited from their constituents. This raises the question of how the internal structure of a bound state can provide a microscopic origin of exciton topology. Here we show that the real-space embedding of charge-transfer excitons can generate an intrinsic manifold of symmetry-related off-site composite orbitals whose coupling supports topological exciton bands. Lateral electron-hole separation embeds the localized exciton on the bond connecting its constituent sites rather than on either site. We demonstrate this mechanism in a honeycomb lattice, where three bond-centered charge-transfer exciton orbitals form a Kagome lattice. By solving the Bethe-Salpeter equation, we show that this emergent multi-orbital manifold supports a topological exciton flat band upon time-reversal symmetry breaking, even when the electron and hole bands are topologically trivial. The resulting band exhibits nearly uniformly distributed quantum geometry, favorable for interaction-driven bosonic states. Our results establish a general route toward topological bands of localized composite bound states and unconventional strongly correlated bosonic phases.

### Paper [232]
Title: Autonomous Optimization of Complex Oxides for Thermochemical Fuel Production
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06877
Authors: Shuiping Gong, Mingcheng Li, Han Hao, Zhenhao Zhou, Yi Li, Xiaobo Liao, Cheng Fang, Jian Deng, Jiangang He, Wenpei Gao, Yakun Yuan, Tao Deng, Chaochao Dun, Runxia Cai, Zhenpeng Yao
Abstract: Two-step thermochemical cycles for H2O and CO2 splitting offer a promising route to sustainable fuel production. Their performance depends on redox-active oxides that undergo cyclic reduction and oxidation reactions. Achieving high thermal-to-fuel conversion efficiency requires both favorable redox thermodynamics and sufficiently rapid reaction kinetics. These requirements have shifted materials design toward complex multication oxides, including mixed-cation fluorites, perovskites, and high-entropy oxides. Developing such materials requires the simultaneous optimization of their composition, defect chemistry, phase stability, and morphology. The resulting design space is difficult to explore through sequential trial and error. Thermochemical fuel production therefore provides a compelling testbed for autonomous materials design and optimization. In this study, we first examine why redox-active complex oxides are challenging to develop owing to their multidimensional phase spaces, harsh operating conditions, and competing functional targets. We then discuss how high-throughput computation, automated synthesis, characterization, and testing, together with machine learning, can be integrated into closed-loop workflows to address these challenges. Drawing on advances from broader oxide materials research, we define a capability roadmap encompassing complex-oxide synthesis, operando characterization, robotic testing, condition-aware computation, and multi-objective optimization. Finally, we identify the key experimental, computational, data, and artificial intelligence capabilities required to build self-improving optimization platforms for materials development in thermochemical fuel production

### Paper [233]
Title: Resolving the Gibbs Paradox through Local Free Space and Collision Potential
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08457
Authors: Chuang Li
Abstract: The Gibbs paradox is conventionally resolved by invoking the indistinguishability of identical particles and the associated $N!$ correction, while the standard entropy of mixing remains independent of molecular properties. Here, we develop a classical framework that does not rely on quantum indistinguishability. We treat molecules initially occupying different spatial regions as distinguishable and describe the accessible volume of each molecule by its instantaneous local free space. We further account for the non-negligible time-averaged potential energy associated with molecular collisions and rederive the entropy increment of gas mixing within the canonical ensemble. The resulting expression depends explicitly on molecular properties, including molecular mass, effective radius, and characteristic collision time, and therefore varies among different gases while yielding zero entropy increment for identical gases naturally. This framework provides a novel classical resolution of the Gibbs paradox by connecting the entropy of mixing to molecular properties rather than to an additional quantum assumption or some new conceptual explanation.

### Paper [234]
Title: Random close packing at extreme size ratios with an Adam-based inflation protocol
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12235
Authors: Kenneth W. Desmond
Abstract: We present \texttt{rcpgenerator}, an openly available code for generating $d$-dimensional dense, disordered, non-overlapping close packings from an arbitrary prescribed list of particle diameters. The method adapts the Clarke--Wiley inflation protocol, but instead uses the Adam optimizer to relax the particle configuration. Typically, particle coordinates are advanced with a single, global step size, which must shrink as the size ratio $S\equiv D_{\max}/D_{\min}$ grows, generally stalling the optimization. Adam instead gives each coordinate its own adaptive step size, stabilizing the optimization time across a broader range of $S$. We demonstrate this in three-dimensional periodic tests that reach $S\sim5\times10^{5}$ for a continuous lognormal distribution ($N\sim10^{6}$ diameters) and particle numbers up to $N\approx5.6\times10^{6}$ for power-law distributions, with the densest packings reaching $\phi\simeq0.87$, each completed in minutes to hours on a multicore machine. Across truncated-lognormal, truncated-power-law, and Weibull distributions, the resulting $\phi$ reproduces trends such as the locations of peaks and knees with distribution shape and $S$ found in prior numerical results and in the parameter-free Farr--Groot prediction, with a remaining offset typically $0.005$--$0.01$. Additionally, results are commensurate with multimodal packing densities measured in vibrated-bed experiments. The code and the complete per-case census behind every figure are released with the paper.

### Paper [235]
Title: Equivariant learning of a transferable three-dimensional classical density functional
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13506
Authors: Bingqing Cheng
Abstract: Liquids exhibit collective behavior that depends sensitively on thermodynamic conditions, interfaces and confinement, yet predicting each new state commonly requires a separate atomistic simulation. Classical density functional theory offers a reusable variational description, but its central excess free-energy functional is generally unknown, and learned approximations for fluids have largely been developed in reduced-dimensional geometries. Here we show that this functional can be learned directly from fully three-dimensional canonical equilibrium density fields while preserving spatial symmetry and variational consistency, without free-energy or chemical-potential training labels. A functional learned from coarse three-dimensional density fields remains predictive at finer resolution, separating the sampling demands of training from the spatial resolution of subsequent predictions. A single learned functional transfers across temperatures, system sizes and statistical ensembles, and recovers the equation of state, liquid--vapor coexistence and interfacial broadening, none of which are used as training targets. Applied to complex three-dimensional geometries, it predicts the potential of mean force between colloids due to solvent mediation, and adsorption isotherms in an interconnected gyroid pore. These results demonstrate that molecular dynamics equilibrium density data can be converted into a transferable thermodynamic generator connecting microscopic liquid structure to response, phase behavior and collective phenomena.

### Paper [236]
Title: Geometric Propulsion of a Scallop with Control-Dependent Hydrodynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24158
Authors: Hisao Hayakawa
Abstract: We study a force-free single-hinge swimmer driven by two controls. When its axial hydrodynamic connection depends only on the opening angle, we show that the mean displacement per cycle vanishes in the periodic state at any driving rate. An independent control of the hydrodynamic response removes this restriction. For a factorized connection, the displacement at arbitrary driving rate is an integral of a measurable shape moment against the hydrodynamic modulation; in the slow-driving limit it reduces to a curvature flux which, at fixed potential and connection, is independent of the shape mobility. A reflecting harmonic model illustrates how thermal fluctuations modify this flux without generating it, and a hinged-bead model with variable passive slip gives a quantitative opening--closing test of the mechanism. We distinguish these propulsion results from the leading shape-sector dissipation, which depends on the mobility and is governed by a thermodynamic metric.

### Paper [237]
Title: Approximating thermodynamic equilibrium observables of quasi one-dimensional magnetic molecules and chains by thermal DMRG - a user perspective
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24608
Authors: L. Horstmann (Bielefeld University), J. Schnack (Bielefeld University)
Abstract: Density matrix renormalization group methods or tensor network methods in general can not only be used to determine ground states but also to evaluate thermal equilibrium properties. Although convergence is superior for one-dimensional quantum spin systems with nearest neighbor exchange and open boundary conditions, the hope is that these methods can as well be employed to approximate magnetic observables of magnetic molecules with more complex interaction patterns. Here, we assume the position of a user and study the accuracy that can realistically be achieved for several archetypical quasi one-dimensional structures using the TenPy suite. In our study, we aim at systems that are too large for exact diagonalization or Krylov space methods.

### Paper [238]
Title: Exact pair density wave in topological moire flat bands and universal superfluid stiffness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26245
Authors: Zhengzhi Wu, Ming-rui Li, Hong Yao
Abstract: Pair-density waves (PDWs) are unconventional superconducting states beyond the BCS paradigm, but identifying them unambiguously in microscopic lattice models is generally challenging due to various competing orders. In particular, an exact realization of PDW in interacting models with topological bands remains elusive. Here we construct an interacting model on a twisted bilayer checkerboard lattice (TBCB) featuring topological flat bands, and rigorously show that an exact PDW ground state can be induced by quantum geometric nesting (QGN), owing to its momentum-space nonsymmorphic symmetry. We further prove that the superfluid stiffness of any nondegenerate QGN superconductor (including the PDW studied here and uniform superconductor) obeys the universal relation $D_s=2N_{\rm flat}\nu(1-\nu)m_{\rm pair}^{-1}$, where $N_{\rm flat}$, $\nu$, and $m_{\rm pair}$ are the number of flat bands, flat band filling, and the two-particle effective mass, respectively. Our results reveal a rich interplay among PDW order, quantum geometry, and band topology.

### Paper [239]
Title: Evaluating LLM-based AI agents integrated with materials synthesis tools: the case of atomic layer deposition
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29309
Authors: Angel Yanguas-Gil
Abstract: This work provides an overview of the different strategies that can be used to evaluate the performance of AI models and agents based on large language models (LLMs) for materials synthesis. After providing a brief overview of the key technologies behind the current generation of AI agents based on LLMs, we summarize the different approaches to evaluating these models in the context of materials science and in particular on materials synthesis, with a specific emphasis on scenarios in which the models are directly integrated with experimental tools. We discuss evaluation strategies spanning knowledge and reasoning benchmarks, tool-use benchmarks, and closed loop benchmarks involving the interaction with experimental systems or realistic virtual tools. We use atomic layer deposition (ALD) as a case study, emphasizing how existing approaches in the literature both build from general approaches used beyond materials science and can be generalized to other materials synthesis techniques. Finally, we provide a practical evaluation framework to evaluate LLMs in the context of materials synthesis

### Paper [240]
Title: Liquid-liquid phase transitions in dipolar liquids. Insights into Supercooled Water
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02666
Authors: Maria Grazia Izzo
Abstract: Dipolar liquids combine two coupled degrees of freedom, translational and dipolar. Intriguingly, these two sectors can reside in distinct thermodynamic states, with the state of one sector possibly favouring a specific state of the other. The onset of ferroelectric order in dipolar liquids is an example of this mutual stabilization. Since this coupling is often encoded in interaction potentials, projecting out one sector to derive an effective interaction for the other can uncover the microscopic mechanism underlying this phenomenon. A coarse-grained theory, solved in the large-dimensional limit, in which the distance between nearest-neighbour particles depends on their dipolar interaction through a density-dependent mechanical compliance shows that a ferroelectric transition can trigger a liquid-liquid transition. It also predicts a first-order liquid-liquid transition within the paraelectric phase that, in turn, can promote ferroelectric ordering. The anisotropic dipolar-dependent soft-core interaction is shown to lead to an effective Jagla-like isotropic two-length-scale interaction, known to support liquid-liquid phase transitions. Here, however, the two-length-scale pattern depends on density and dipolar configuration, and is therefore sensitive to dipolar order. Analysis of supercooled TIP4P/Ice water molecular dynamics simulations shows that first-coordination-shell features of the pair correlation functions are sensitive to dipolar interaction, supporting the foundations of the coarse-grained theory. Comparison of low- and high-density liquid water reveals enhanced spatial anisotropy of the first coordination shell in the low-density liquid, associated, within the theory, with increased effective excluded volume, showing that the liquid-liquid transition involves structural changes within the first shell, beyond reorganization of the interstitial region and tetrahedral order.

### Paper [241]
Title: Composition-dependent nonlinear viscoelastic-viscoplastic behavior and constitutive framework for digitally mixed polymers spanning the glass transition
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04399
Authors: Beijun Shen, Mary C. Boyce
Abstract: Multi-material PolyJet printing produces voxel-scale digital mixtures of an elastomeric photopolymer (Agilus) and a glassy photopolymer (Vero), giving a material family whose room-temperature response ranges from elastomeric to glassy. Large-deformation uniaxial compression over nearly three orders of magnitude in strain rate reveals a nonlinear, rate-dependent load-unload response that evolves continuously with composition, from recoverable elastomeric hysteresis to glassy yield with post-yield softening, hardening, and substantial residual strain. Dynamic mechanical analysis (DMA) shows that each mixture has a single glass transition temperature ($T_g$) that shifts to higher temperature with both Vero fraction and frequency, so composition acts much as temperature or loading rate does. This time-composition equivalence motivates one constitutive structure for the whole family: an equilibrium hyperelastic network with three non-equilibrium, rate-dependent branches carrying reptational, intermolecular, and glassy resistance. Its properties are anchored at the two endpoints and interpolated by smooth composition scaling laws. The model captures the compression response of all seven calibrated compositions and predicts a withheld mixture from the scaling laws alone, as well as the plane-strain compression and uniaxial tension responses of three representative compositions. Resolving the predicted stress into its branches shows the load passing from the elastomeric to the glassy mechanism, and the work from elastic storage to dissipation, as Vero content and rate rise. The glass transition that DMA maps at small strain therefore governs the large-strain deformation mechanisms. The framework gives a compact, physically based description of digitally mixed polymers and a predictive basis for designing functionally graded, architected multi-material structures.

### Paper [242]
Title: Positive Temperature Coefficient of Anisotropy due to $d^6L$ Groundstate in $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08927
Authors: Rachel Nickel, Kurt Kummer, Johan van Lierop
Abstract: Positive anisotropy temperature coefficients ($dK/dT>0$) usually arise from hybridization between magnetic 3d states and strongly spin-orbit-coupled (SOC) subsystems. Yet $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ shows $dK/dT>0$ (125-200~K) without an obvious SOC partner. We study pure and Cr-doped $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ across the transition from low temperature incommensurate to high-anisotropy magnetic phases. We identify a $d^6L$ groundstate in $\epsilon$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ while reduced hybridization yields $d^6 + d^6L^2$ in the Cr-doped phase, highlighting metal-ligand hybridization as a route to tune magnetic, electronic and orbitronic properties.

### Paper [243]
Title: Effects of Interaction Range on Fluid Multicriticality: A Computational Study of an Interconverting Lattice Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09074
Authors: Thomas J. Longo, Sergey V. Buldyrev, Fr\'ed\'eric Caupin, Mikhail A. Anisimov
Abstract: The range of intermolecular interactions plays a central role in determining the nature of phase behavior and critical phenomena. It is well established through studies of the Ising model that as interaction range increases, Monte Carlo simulations progressively approach meanfield predictions as the effects of critical fluctuations are suppressed. In this work, we investigate how varying interaction range influences fluid multicriticality using an interconverting lattice model that exhibits both Ising-like liquid-gas criticality and symmetric fluid tricriticality (similar to that in the superfluid $^4$He-$^3$He mixture). This minimal model serves as a representative system for exploring the evolution of competing critical points within a generic framework. We analyze the model using both meanfield theory and three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulations while systematically varying the number of interacting neighbors, $Z_n$, from 6 to 388 on a simple cubic lattice. We find that the system with nearest-neighbor interactions ($Z_n=6$) reveals only two types of multicritical behavior, while for larger interaction ranges, four distinct archetypes emerge. We demonstrate the convergence of the simulation results to those of the meanfield theory as the number of interacting neighbors tends to infinity, and we discuss the results within the framework of crossover critical phenomena.

### Paper [244]
Title: Heteroepitaxial Growth of PbSe on InP via III-V Buffer Layers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09605
Authors: Biridiana Rodriguez, Mark Martino, Brody Yeung, Benjamin Sprenger, Leland Nordin
Abstract: Detector cost remains a barrier to the widespread adoption of mid-wave infrared (3-5 micron) technology. PbSe, an inexpensive narrow band gap IV-VI semiconductor that has been used since the early 1940s, delivers high performance for infrared detection despite the abundance of grain boundaries in polycrystalline films. Epitaxial growth, however, could provide superior crystalline quality and interfaces, but suitable substrates remain limited for PbSe. Recent PbSe heteroepitaxy has focused primarily on III-V, II-VI and group-IV substrates that each offer a comparatively narrow range of lattice-matched alloys for heterostructure engineering. Here we show that InP-based heteroepitaxy provides access to a broader materials platform while limiting the lattice mismatch with PbSe to approximately 4%. We grow 151-nm thick PbSe films by molecular beam epitaxy on 200-nm thick In0.53Ga0.47As and In0.52Al0.48As buffers on (001) InP substrates. Reflection high-energy electron diffraction and X-ray diffraction show (001)-oriented rock-salt PbSe with an out-of-plane lattice constant of 6.12 Angstrom on both buffers. Photoluminescence (PL) is observed from room-temperature down to 12 K and the peak wavelength red-shifts from 3.7 to 5.0 microns. Under identical measurement conditions, the room-temperature peak PL intensities from films on In0.53Ga0.47As and In0.52Al0.48As are approximately 1.9x and 1.3x that of a PbSe on GaAs substrate reference, respectively. These results establish an InP-compatible platform for integrating narrow band-gap PbSe with a broad range of ternary and quaternary III-V alloys, including, for example, structures in which In0.53Ga0.47As serves as both a short-wave infrared absorber and a template for PbSe growth.

### Paper [245]
Title: Stripe-Like Superconducting Enhancement and Coexisting Magnetic Texture in an Infinite Layer Nickelate
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15958
Authors: Ryan Laing, Dung Vu, Jacob Pfund, Wenzheng Wei, Frederick J. Walker, Haiyan Tan, Pavel Volkov, Rodrigo Castillo-Garza, Menka Jain, Charles Ahn, Ilya Sochnikov
Abstract: Despite their promise as structural and electronic analogs to cuprates, nickelate thin films consistently exhibit broadened superconducting transitions in many experiments that remain poorly understood. Local measurements are ideal for revealing the presence of defects or competing phases, which may cause this transition broadening through a phase-separation. Here, we use scanning SQUID microscopy to investigate local superfluid density and magnetic texture of optimally doped $\mathrm{Nd}_{1-x}\mathrm{Eu}_{x}\mathrm{NiO}_{2}$ (NENO) (x=0.25) grown via molecular beam epitaxy. We observed a weakly-magnetic texture coexisting with superconductivity. Spatially resolved susceptibility imaging reveals a highly non-uniform superconducting state, characterized by a robust stripe-like enhancement pattern appearing near the phase transition. By resolving the mesoscopic landscape of electronic and magnetic inhomogeneities, these findings suggest that a competing magnetic phase is a primary contributor to the unusually broad superconducting transitions of these optimally doped NENO samples. Understanding the uncovered superconducting enhancement may provide a path to raising superconducting temperatures in these materials.

### Paper [246]
Title: Higher-Order Interactions in Complex Systems: Mechanisms, Behaviour, Representation and Reducibility
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20142
Authors: Francisco J. P\'erez-Reche
Abstract: Higher-order interactions couple three or more elements in ways not decomposable into pairwise contributions. They are associated with phenomenological signatures, collective thermodynamic or dynamical properties such as explosive synchronization and discontinuous transitions. Neither such signatures nor a many-body description establishes a many-body mechanism, since both depend on how the system is represented. Connecting work on higher-order network dynamics and multivariate statistical dependence, this review distinguishes the mechanism governing a system, the behaviour it produces and how either is represented. Behaviour comprises phenomenological signatures and statistical dependence. Representation covers which variables are included and how relations are encoded. We examine direct many-body mechanisms, nonlinear but pairwise mechanisms producing similar signatures, indirect routes through shared environments or eliminated variables, and temporal aggregation, an artefact of observation. The same behaviour can arise from different mechanisms, and a higher-order mechanism has no universal behavioural consequence. We formulate reducibility, whether a higher-order model can be replaced by a lower-order one, relative to an admissible model class, a target and a tolerance, and apply it to synchronization, ecology, statistical mechanics and contagion. Information-theoretic and inferential methods are reviewed for detecting higher-order dependence, testing lower-order alternatives and reconstructing interaction structure. What data can establish about the model is a question of identifiability, distinct from reducibility. Observational data alone cannot uniquely establish the mechanism without structural assumptions, resolved dynamics or interventions. We close with open problems in linking mechanisms to measurable signatures, coarse-graining, identifiability and causality. (Abridged)

### Paper [247]
Title: Functional Renormalization for Random Matrix Theory: The relational background field method
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21031
Authors: Vincent Lahoche, Dine Ousmane Samary
Abstract: The construction of a reliable renormalization group (RG) flow for discrete gravity models that preserves their underlying symmetry group, typically $U(N)$ or $O(N)$, remains an open problem. For random matrix models, which are the focus of this paper, this symmetry is intrinsically tied to the interactions encoding the random geometry of two-dimensional quantum Euclidean spacetime. We develop a novel approach based on the introduction of a partial matrix-valued intermediate field. In the large-$N$ limit, measure concentration strongly suppresses fluctuations of its singular values, allowing it to play the role of a self-consistent background field. This provides the basis for a relational RG in which the notion of scale is dynamically induced by the effective Gaussian measure in the basis where the intermediate field is diagonal. Our construction preserves the symmetry of the original model and admits a well-defined continuum limit. We show that the resulting infrared theory is described by a three-dimensional non-local Euclidean field theory with a non-trivial Wilson-Fisher-like fixed point and a single relevant direction. Remarkably, the associated critical exponent exactly matches the standard double-scaling exponent. We finally discuss extensions of the background-field approach to other discrete gravity models, such as random tensor models, and to different symmetry groups, as well as connections with more formal RG frameworks and information geometry.

### Paper [248]
Title: Stochastic optimisation method for estimating large deviations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24473
Authors: Dani\"el W. H. Cloete, Hugo Touchette
Abstract: A stochastic optimisation method was recently proposed to efficiently compute large deviation functions, used in statistical physics to characterise the fluctuations of nonequilibrium systems, modelled as Markov processes. The method, called LDSTOP, combines numerical techniques from control theory and machine learning to iteratively construct the "driven process", a controlled version of the Markov process used as a model of nonequilibrium system that realises a given fluctuation or large deviation of that system in an optimal way. Compared to other methods based on spectral approximations, importance sampling, cloning or splitting, LDSTOP is simple, flexible, and scalable, as it works by simulating single trajectories that are gradually guided towards the driven process. Here, we illustrate these advantages by applying the method on the full range of Markov processes considered in applications, namely, discrete-time Markov chains, continuous-time jump processes, and diffusion processes. For each type, we define the objective function to be optimised, explain different options available for representing the driven process (using, e.g., neural networks), and provide implementation details through simple applications. With these contributions, we aim to showcase the method's efficiency, as well as its ease of use when combined with available machine learning packages, such as PyTorch, TensorFlow and JAX, for simulating stochastic processes and solving high-dimensional optimisation problems.

### Paper [249]
Title: Critical escape dynamics of an active particle moving on curved surfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30455
Authors: Maxim Root, Hartmut L\"owen, Peter Sollich, Lorenzo Caprini, Alexander P. Antonov
Abstract: On the macroscopic scale, active (self-propelled) objects moving through complex environments are often subject to effects arising from both the curvature of the surrounding surface and external influences such as gravity. We study the Kramers escape problem for active Brownian particles in two settings: motion in an external potential driven solely by gradient forces, and motion on a curved manifold subject to an additional constant gravitational force. In the absence of translational noise, a sharp dynamical transition occurs when the self-propulsion velocity $v_0$ reaches a critical velocity $v_c$ required for an escape. Above this threshold, the escape rate $k$ follows a universal scaling form, $k \propto \exp\left[-\mathrm{const}(v_0-v_c)^{-\gamma}\right]$, with an exponent $\gamma=3/2$ for the escape in a potential and $\gamma=1/2$ for the escape on a curved manifold in the presence of gravity. These distinct exponents reveal how the interplay of activity, surface curvature, and gravity determines the critical escape dynamics. Our theoretical predictions are verified by numerical simulations.

### Paper [250]
Title: t-J model at 50
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35178
Authors: J\'ozef Spa{\l}ek
Abstract: I briefly overview the original formulation of the t-J model starting from Hubbard model, as well as stress its unique features, i.e., advantages and shortcomings when discussing its physical properties. Particular emphasis is put on a brief characterization of connection between magnetism, unconventional superconductivity, and the Mott-Hubbard localization of the correlated carriers in a narrow band. In the second part I summarize the model generalization to the three--orbital situation. The main purpose is to list some basic features of the physics associated with t--J model.

### Paper [251]
Title: Finite-momentum trimers and Cooper quartets in a one-dimensional Fermi gas with coexistent $s$- and $p$-wave interactions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37032
Authors: Yixin Guo
Abstract: We study finite-momentum trimers and Cooper quartets in a one-dimensional two-component Fermi gas with coexistent $s$- and $p$-wave interactions, regularized in the relative momentum of each pair so that they remain Galilean invariant. Pauli blocking alone then makes the trimer dispersion deviate from free center-of-mass motion, so the trimer energy can be lowest at finite total momentum. Allowing this motion expands the $abb$ region among pairs and trimers and removes the region where an odd-wave pair is lowest. The $aabb$ quartet lies below all these branches at every sampled coupling, and in a strip starting on the crossing of the two dimer-pair channels its lowest breakup channel is a moving $abb$ trimer and an atom. At weak interspecies attraction, a variational bound shows that two odd-wave Cooper pairs bind into a quartet, whose binding exceeds that of the coexisting trimer by orders of magnitude as the attraction weakens. At stronger interspecies attraction, the quartet binding relative to two unlike-species dimers disappears when the odd-wave interaction is switched off, although the even-wave interaction dominates the interaction energy. Relative to the in-vacuum case, the Fermi sea enhances the weak-coupling quartet binding, and at fixed scattering lengths the quartet binding grows with density while the coexisting trimer becomes much shallower. These results show that neither the most strongly bound pair nor the dominant interaction energy alone determines whether the quartet binds.

### Paper [252]
Title: Diffusion-controlled reactions with time-dependent surface reactivity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23017
Authors: Denis S. Grebenkov, Yilin Ye
Abstract: We investigate diffusion-controlled reactions on a reactive surface with time-dependent reactivity. Starting from a conventional formulation via diffusion equation with Robin boundary condition for the macroscopic concentration of particles, we aim at quantifying how temporal variations of reactivity affect the survival probability and the associated first-reaction time (FRT) of a single particle on the reactive surface. Using an adiabatic description, we first identify five long-time regimes of the first-reaction time distribution, ranging from exponential and stretched-exponential to power-law decay. In particular, sufficiently rapid decay of the reactivity leads to a nonzero probability of never reacting, despite diffusion in a bounded domain. Second, we provide a probabilistic reformulation of this problem and compare it with a different scenario of encounter-dependent reactivity. We use a heuristic mapping to compare the long-time kinetics of time-dependent and encounter-dependent reactivity. Our theoretical predictions are validated via extensive Monte Carlo simulations.

### Paper [253]
Title: Bayesian Inference in Partially Observed Critical Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35973
Authors: Mihika Singhania
Abstract: We ask how much of a large random system must be observed to infer a global average with controlled uncertainty. For the random transposition shuffle, a conjugate Bayesian estimator of the average displacement, computed from $m$ sampled cards, has relative uncertainty $1/\sqrt{2m}$, independent of the deck size $n$. Because the configuration is a single draw from equilibrium, a floor $R_n$ remains, and no relative tolerance below $\kappa\sqrt{R_n}$ can be certified, even by a census. For the shuffle $R_n=(2n^2+7)/\bigl(5(n-1)^2(n+1)\bigr)$. Before equilibrium, a certificate built on the displacement loses sight after about $\frac n4\ln m$ steps, half as long as the count of fixed cards in the same sample. At the two-dimensional Ising critical point the floor of the energy vanishes, whereas that of the magnetisation stays near $0.072$, with mean-field limit $\sqrt2-1$. For correlated fields, the usual 95\% interval can cover the ensemble mean in only 7\% of cases, and no bound on the floor from one realisation holds under stationarity alone.

### Paper [254]
Title: Weak-coupling selection and finite-torus entropy in the triangular Ising antiferromagnet
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00154
Authors: Weiqi Jiang, Pengcheng Hou
Abstract: An antiferromagnetic second-neighbor interaction favors stripe order in the triangular-lattice Ising antiferromagnet, but uniform stripes cannot close when both primitive periods are odd. At each fixed odd size $L\geq5$, we minimize this interaction over the ground states of the zero-field nearest-neighbor model on an $L\times L$ torus. We prove a minimum total interface length of $4L$ and classify all $L^2(L^2-1)/2$ selected states. Under the uniform measure on these states, the area fractions of the three stripe orientations approach a uniform distribution over all nonnegative triples summing to one. Thus selection reduces an exponential degeneracy to polynomial growth, while the whole-sample orientation fractions remain broadly distributed. We also derive explicit two-sided entropy-density bounds for periodic spin ground states and honeycomb dimer coverings on $M\times N$ tori, where $M$ and $N$ are the periods along the two primitive lattice directions. For all $M,N\geq3$, the errors relative to the bulk residual entropy are bounded by explicit constants times $\min(M,N)^{-2}$, without an aspect-ratio restriction. These bounds give finite-size accuracy guarantees and control the exponentially small fraction of unperturbed ground states that survive the selection.

### Paper [255]
Title: First-principles study of the formation of Group-IV--vacancy color centers in diamond
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06671
Authors: Xiangru Han, Adam Gali
Abstract: $XV$ ($X$ is Si, Ge, Sn, or Pb, and $V$ is the carbon vacancy) color centers are promising platforms for quantum computing and quantum information science due to their favorable optical and spin properties. They are commonly fabricated by ion implantation, which inevitably introduces vacancies and carbon self-interstitials (C$_i$) into the lattice. However, the microscopic formation mechanism remains unclear, limiting the yield and stability of $XV$ centers. Here, first-principles calculations are conducted to systematically investigate the roles of $V$ and C$_i$ in $XV$ formation. We find that $XV$ formation is energetically favorable, with large binding energies and low diffusion barriers. Nevertheless, $XV$ readily captures C$_i$ to form substitutional defect $X_s$, or captures an additional $V$ to form the complex $XV_2$. Charge transition levels show that oppositely charged reactants never meet in these reactions, and that Coulomb repulsion blocks them only when both reactants carry charges of the same sign, which for the capture of C$_i$ requires strongly n- or p-type conditions. These competing defect reactions destabilize the split-vacancy configuration and could account for the low formation yield of $XV$ centers, so that they appear at concentrations well below those expected from the implanted dose. Our results elucidate the microscopic origin of this intrinsic instability and suggest that Fermi-level control may suppress defect conversions and stabilize the split-vacancy configuration.

### Paper [256]
Title: Heat Transport Mechanisms in MOF-5
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08024
Authors: Zezhu Zeng, Xia Liang, Hengtai Zheng, Aron Walsh, David E. Manolopoulos
Abstract: We find that MOF-5 supports both long-lived phonons and linker vibrations whose split spectra signal a breakdown of the phonon quasiparticle approximation. Combining mode-projected molecular dynamics spectra with spectral Wigner heat transport, we resolve two contrasting transport channels: low-frequency particle-like phonon propagation decreases strongly from 200 to 500 K, whereas wave-like phonon coherence remains nearly constant and supplies about half the intrinsic conductivity at 500 K. A phenomenological extrinsic scattering model provides a plausible explanation for the discrepancy between calculated and measured conductivities in terms of both its magnitude and temperature dependence. We also identify signatures of phonon quasiparticle breakdown in the linker vibrations of ZIF-8 and HKUST-1, indicating that this feature extends to other metal-organic frameworks.

### Paper [257]
Title: Native vacancies and antisites in rock-salt and wurtzite MnSe: formation energies across the growth window and spin splitting in the A-type order
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08986
Authors: Tom\'as Rojas Solorzano (CICIMA, Universidad de Costa Rica, Sede de Occidente, Universidad de Costa Rica), Dale Igram (University of Michigan-Dearborn)
Abstract: Manganese selenide grows both as rock-salt $\alpha$-MnSe, a type-II antiferromagnet, and as metastable wurtzite w-MnSe, an antiferromagnet whose type of order is not known. In DFT+$U$, a spin-degenerate stripe order of w-MnSe lies 14 meV per formula unit below the A-type order, which is predicted to be altermagnetic. We compute the neutral Mn and Se vacancies and the two antisites of both polymorphs with a linear-response Hubbard $U$, between the Se-rich limit bounded by MnSe$_2$ and the Mn-rich limit set by the measured formation enthalpy. In $\alpha$-MnSe the Mn vacancy is lowest over most of the window (0.92 eV under Se-rich conditions), a candidate acceptor for its p-type conduction. In w-MnSe the Se antisite is lowest under Se-rich conditions, narrowly in the A-type order and, by an estimate, more clearly in the stripe order, and the Se vacancy under Mn-rich conditions. Changing the order shifts the formation energies by up to 0.26 eV, most for the Mn vacancy. Only the Se vacancy has nearly the same formation energy in both polymorphs; the other defects differ by 0.4-1.1 eV, although DFT+$U$ places wurtzite below rock salt, contrary to experiment. Three of the four defects leave an uncompensated moment of 3-5 $\mu_\mathrm{B}$. In the A-type order, band unfolding shows that the sign-reversing splitting of the most strongly split valence band, about 230 meV and 3 eV below the valence-band maximum (band-edge states are not resolved), changes its peak height by less than 8%, the resolution of the analysis, at 3% defects, while elsewhere the curve deviates by 18-45 meV (rms).

### Paper [258]
Title: Theory of Optical and Magneto-Optical Properties of [111]-Oriented Site-Controlled Quantum Dots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09704
Authors: N. D. O'Connor, G. Juska, E. Pelucchi, S. Schulz
Abstract: Site-controlled [111]-oriented quantum dots (QDs) are promising sources of non-classical light, yet their optical spectra exhibit apparently conflicting symmetry signatures. At zero magnetic field, excitonic spectra are commonly interpreted as $C_{2v}$-like, whereas measurements in Faraday geometry display selection rules characteristic of $C_{3v}$-like symmetry. Here, we show that both regimes can be described consistently without invoking any change in the underlying structural symmetry. Combining 8-band $\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{p}$ theory, a self-consistent Hartree-informed configuration interaction scheme, and group theoretical analysis, we introduce a controlled symmetry reducing perturbation that lowers the QD symmetry to $C_s$. At zero field, the optical response remains predominantly $C_{2v}$-like because the additional low-symmetry transitions carry only weak oscillator strengths. In Faraday geometry, the same reduced-symmetry structure produces four dominant, nearly circularly polarised trion transitions, reproducing the experimentally observed $C_{3v}$-like response. By contrast, an exact $C_{2v}$ description inferred from the zero-field spectrum permits only two such trion transitions and therefore fails at finite field. Our results thus provide a unified description of the zero and finite magnetic field optical response without any restoration or enhancement of the underlying structural symmetry. More generally, we demonstrate that a higher-symmetry-like optical spectrum does not uniquely identify the exact symmetry of a QD, therefore also providing useful guidance for other QD and nanostructure systems that lie beyond the ones studied here.

### Paper [259]
Title: Energy- and momentum-resolved Cooper-pair emission from a cuprate superconductor
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09857
Authors: Henry Amir, Jack Zwettler, Ka Ho Wong, Genda Gu, Rafael M. Fernandes, Jorge Noronha, Dirk K. Morr, Fahad Mahmood
Abstract: Superconductivity arises from Cooper pairing, but conventional photoemission probes electrons individually rather than as pairs. Here we report the observation of Cooper-pair emission from an optimally doped cuprate superconductor using two-electron angle-resolved photoemission (2e-ARPES). Below the superconducting transition, we observe a distinct two-electron emission peak at zero pair binding energy and zero pair momentum that disappears in the normal state, revealing the characteristic fingerprint of a Cooper pair. Unexpectedly, the joint energy distribution broadens markedly along the relative-energy axis as temperature decreases, while the total energy width remains nearly unchanged. This anomalous behavior cannot be explained by instrumental resolution, static disorder or thermal broadening, and instead points to scattering processes involving collective excitations of the superconductor. Our results establish 2e-ARPES as a probe of both Cooper-pair emission and the many-body dynamics of the superconducting state.

### Paper [260]
Title: Dynamics of R\'enyi entanglement entropies and Eigenstate Thermalization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13141
Authors: Riccardo Senese, Pasquale Calabrese
Abstract: How is entanglement dynamics encoded in the eigenstates of the time-evolution operator? We address this question by focusing on R\'enyi entropy dynamics in deterministic chaotic Floquet models in arbitrary dimensions. Inspired by the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH), we employ spectral representations of reduced density matrix moments (purity and its higher-order generalizations) whose decay through spectral dephasing captures nonequilibrium entanglement growth. These representations closely resemble those governing local-observable dynamics, but involve eigenstates in a replicated Hilbert space and observables are replaced by subsystem permutation operators, such as the swap operator for R\'enyi-2. Focusing on R\'enyi-2, we combine exact random matrix theory calculations, established ETH results and numerical tests in 1D to formulate ETH-like ans\"atze for subsystem swap matrix elements in the replicated eigenbasis and their cross-correlations with initial-state overlap statistics. In the thermodynamic limit, purity dynamics is entirely determined by smooth slowly varying functions that emerge from such statistics, paralleling generalized ETH formulations in a single replica. In the limit of large subsystems, these functions exhibit a striking structural simplification: locality of the dynamics together with bulk spectral dephasing reduce their functional dependence to a single fundamental frequency. Our formalism provides a setting where ETH-like statements naturally coexist for both arbitrarily small and large subsystem sizes. We discuss extensions of our results to higher R\'enyi entropies. Beyond clarifying the spectral foundations of entanglement dynamics, these results lay some of the groundwork for future applications of spectral filter methods to the computation of R\'enyi evolution in deterministic systems.

### Paper [261]
Title: Thermodynamic Properties of the S=1/2 Zigzag Spin Ladder
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13895
Authors: Nobuya Maeshima, Kouichi Okunishi, Toshiya Hikihara
Abstract: We investigate finite-temperature properties of the spin-1/2 zigzag Heisenberg ladder with an anisotropic off-diagonal $\Gamma$ interaction. Thermal states are calculated by an infinite time-evolving block decimation method combined with purification, while exact diagonalization is used to analyze low-lying excitations. The entropy is strongly suppressed at low temperatures inside the gapped phases, whereas it remains enhanced near several ground-state phase boundaries. For the N\'eel phase at $J_2/J_1=0$, the model can be mapped by spin rotations onto the exactly solvable XYZ chain. The thermal gap extracted from the low-temperature specific heat agrees with the exact one-spinon gap, while the finite-size excitation gap obtained by exact diagonalization is consistent with a two-spinon excitation.

### Paper [262]
Title: Singlet-triplet superconductivity from repulsive interactions in a $p$-wave magnet
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14609
Authors: Hanbit Oh, Laura Classen, Daniel F. Agterberg
Abstract: Motivated by recent proposals of $p$-wave magnets, which host odd-parity spin splitting, we study electrons near Van Hove singularities with local repulsive interactions. We demonstrate how the symmetry of the $p$-wave spin splitting governs the competition among instabilities. Using a parquet renormalization group (pRG) analysis of a simple one-sublattice model of a $p$-wave magnet, we find that superconductivity is the leading instability over the entire phase diagram for purely repulsive interactions. The resulting superconducting state is generically a mixture of spin-singlet and spin-triplet pairing and approaches an equal-weight mixture when $T_c$ is small compared with the energy offset between each Van Hove electron and its inversion partner. Extending the pRG analysis to a more realistic two-sublattice model, we find that superconductivity remains the leading instability, while the sublattice degree of freedom leads to a competition among various singlet-triplet pairing channels and even a finite-momentum equal-spin pair-density wave. Our mechanism applies broadly to odd-parity magnets and opens a natural route to nodal topological superconductivity.

### Paper [263]
Title: Metric random tilings: counted geometric disorder and its diffraction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16730
Authors: Xinyan Wu, Qingqing Ji
Abstract: Random tilings gain entropy through the many ways fixed shapes can meet. What disorder remains possible when every face keeps its neighbours and every edge its direction? In metric random tilings, shared edges take discrete lengths that must close every face. For three equally spaced lengths, four of the eleven uniform tilings admit a number of geometries that grows exponentially with area. On the honeycomb this multiplicity survives even when the boundary and inventory of each flower patch are prescribed. Breaking the equal spacing lowers the estimated honeycomb entropy from about half a Boltzmann constant per face to about a fifth. Shifting all lengths together preserves every pattern and its probability, yet changes the coherent diffraction intensity by nearly three orders of magnitude on matched honeycomb samples. Metric random tilings thus realize bulk disorder at fixed topology and reveal how strongly its diffraction signature can vary at fixed configurational entropy.

### Paper [264]
Title: Basic introduction to Freidlin-Wentzell theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19916
Authors: Jiawei Yan
Abstract: Freidlin-Wentzell theory provides the quantitative foundation for rare, noise-induced transitions in metastable systems, predicting optimal transition paths, the exponential scaling of mean escape times, and the long-term occupancy of competing attractors. We present a pedagogical, physics-oriented review built on Feng-Kurtz's unified Hamiltonian framework: from the infinitesimal generator of a Markov process we derive the Hamiltonian, the Lagrangian and action via Legendre-Fenchel duality, and the quasi-potential. The same formalism covers weak-noise diffusions, Markov jump processes, interacting particle systems, and nonequilibrium stochastic fields. We show how broken detailed balance separates forward and reverse transition paths, and how W-graphs construct multi-attractor landscapes. Finally, we connect the theory to numerical methods, including ordered upwind methods, geometric minimum action algorithms, and neural-network parameterizations, and provide an accompanying GPU-accelerated Python package.

### Paper [265]
Title: Adsorbate-Free Screening of Dissociative Water Adsorption on Nickel Nanoparticles via Graph Neural Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20706
Authors: Marta Monti, Alec Noppe, Olivier Lugier, Maria B. Brands, Bernd Ensing
Abstract: Dissociative water adsorption is a key step in heterogeneous and electrochemical catalysis, but on finite Ni nanosystems the OH$^*$ and H$^*$ fragments can occupy many combinations of surface sites, making exhaustive density functional theory (DFT) screening impractical. We introduce a graph neural network (GNN) framework with an adsorbate-free representation: candidate O- and H-binding sites are defined by sets of Ni atoms (\textit{anchors}), and each site pair is encoded as a graph constructed solely from these anchors and their local Ni environment. Using only MACE-relaxed bare Ni geometries as input, separate GNNs predict the DFT-reference adsorption energies of OH$^*$+H$^*$ configurations and whether the proposed binding sites would be retained upon DFT relaxation, eliminating the need to construct or relax adsorbates during screening. Trained across Ni nanoclusters and Ni(111)/Ni(100) surfaces, the energy model achieves a mean absolute error of 0.031 eV on the internal validation set and demonstrates transferability to unseen Ni$_{55}$, Ni$_{91}$, and Ni$_{130}$ clusters and larger surface cells, particularly for relative-energy ranking, although quantitative accuracy varies across systems. These independent tests also support the retention classifier's use as a conservative filter for selecting configurations likely to preserve their proposed binding sites. Application to Ni nanoparticles containing up to $\sim$3000 atoms identifies favorable co-adsorption motifs and shows that lower O-site coordination generally stabilizes the dissociated state, whereas H-site coordination has a weaker influence. The framework thus offers an efficient route to screening adsorption energetics, with potential extensions to other metals and adsorbate combinations.

### Paper [266]
Title: Axion electrodynamics and the advection-diffusion structure of TEM modes in hollow-conductor waveguides
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22839
Authors: Andr\'e H. Gomes
Abstract: We present a systematic investigation of transverse electromagnetic (TEM) modes in hollow-conductor waveguides filled with a dielectric medium described by axion electrodynamics. While such modes do not propagate in conventional single-conductor waveguides, the coupling of the axion field to an external magnetic field enables their propagation.
